Duality for higher local fields after Kato and Suzuki
Antoine GALET, 2025

Abstract
A field K K is d d -local if there exist fields K = k d , … , k 0 K=k_{d},\dots,k_{0} with k i + 1 k_{i+1} complete discrete valuation with residue field k i k_{i} , and k 0 k_{0} finite of characteristic p p . By work of Deninger and Wingberg, the Galois cohomology of such fields with finite coefficients satisfies a duality generalizing Tate duality when either d = 0 d=0 , char ⁡ k 1 = 0 \characteristic k_{1}=0 or the coefficients have no p p -torsion. Reviewing and synthesizing results of Suzuki and Kato, we obtain p p -torsion duality statements under the weaker assumption that either d ≤ 1 d\leq 1 or char ⁡ k 2 = 0 \characteristic k_{2}=0 , as well as for varieties over K K , where duality is stated in terms of locally compact Hausdorff topologies on the étale cohomology groups. More generally we obtain results for any perfect k 0 k_{0} , endowing the totally unramified cohomology groups of K K with the structure of ind-pro-quasi-algebraic k 0 k_{0} -groups.



Introduction

Higher local fields
are a higher-dimensional generalization of nonarchimedian local fields, classically defined as follows : a field
K
is
d
-local
if there exists a sequence of fields
K=k_{d},\dots,k_{0}
such that
k_{0}
is finite and
k_{i}
is complete discrete valuation with residue field
k_{i-1}
, for
1\leq i\leq d
. It is often not a great sacrifice to allow
k_{0}
to only be
quasi-finite
,
i.e.
perfect with absolute Galois group
\widehat{\mathbb{Z}}
, or even just perfect, and
k_{i+1}
to only be Henselian when
\characteristic k_{i+1}=0
.

The cohomology of a
p
-adic field
K
with coefficients in finite Galois modules satisfies a duality, by Tate’s local duality theorem : namely the cup-product
H^{q}(K,M)\otimes H^{2-q}(K,M^{\vee})\to\Br(K)\cong\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is a perfect pairing of finite groups for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
and finite Galois module
M
, where
M^{\vee}=\Hom(M,\mathbb{G}_{m})
is the Cartier dual. If instead
K
is local of characteristic
p>0
, the same statement holds if
M
has no
p
-torsion. This has a higher local analogue by
[
10
]
(Prop. 1.2) : if
K=k_{d},\dots,k_{1}
is
d
-local with
k_{0}
quasi-finite of exponent characteristic
p
, and
M
is a finite Galois module of order
m
prime to
p
, then we have perfect pairings of finite groups :

H^{q}(K,M)\otimes H^{d+1-q}(K,M^{\vee})\to H^{d+1}(K,\mu_{m}^{\otimes d})\cong\mathbb{Z}/m

where
M^{\vee}=\Hom(M,\mu_{m}^{\otimes d})
is the Tate-twisted Cartier dual of
M
. If
\characteristic k_{1}=0
and
k_{0}
is finite then this holds even if
M
has
p
-torsion, but in general the cohomology of
K
with
p
-torsion coefficients is not finite. In this paper, we consider such pairings with finite coefficients of arbitrary order.

One approach such is by induction on
d
: considering a complete discrete valuation field
L
with residue field
l
, we have Hochschild-Serre spectral sequences of Galois cohomology :

E^{ij}_{2}=H^{i}(l,H^{j}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},-))\Rightarrow H^{i+j}(L,-)

where
L^{\mathrm{ur}}
is the maximal unramified extension of
L
with respect to its discrete valuation, so duality for a
d
-local field
K=k_{d},\dots,k_{0}
reduces to duality for
k_{0}
and for the unramified cohomology functors
R\Gamma(k_{i}^{\mathrm{ur}},-)
for
i\geq 1
. Three cases are known :

a.

When
m\geq 2
is invertible in
l
,
H^{q}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},-)
sends perfect pairings of finite
m
-torsion Galois modules
M\times N\to\mu_{m}^{\otimes i}
over
L
to perfect pairings
H^{r}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},M)\times H^{1-r}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},N)\to H^{1}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},\mu_{m}^{\otimes i})=\mu_{m}^{\otimes(i-1)}
of finite Galois modules over
l
, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. This recovers the statement above for higher local fields.

b.

When
L
has mixed characteristic
(0,p)
and
[l:l^{p}]<+\infty
, Bertapelle and Suzuki
[
2
]
showed that
H^{q}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},\mu_{p^{n}}^{\otimes r})
has the structure of the
relative perfection of an algebraic unipotent (RPAU)

l
-group
R^{q}\Psi(\mu_{p^{n}}^{\otimes r})
. Such groups, when viewed as sheaves on the
relatively perfect
or
relatively perfectly smooth
site of
l
, admit a notion of duality first developed by Kato
[
19
,
20
]
and expanded upon in
[
21
,
2
]
. Kato and Suzuki
[
21
]
obtained perfect pairings
R\Psi(\mu_{p^{n}}^{\otimes r})\otimes^{L}R\Psi(\mu_{p^{n}}^{\otimes(i-r)})\to\nu_{n}(i-1)[-1]
where
p^{i-1}=[l:l^{p}]
and
\nu_{n}(r)=W_{n}\Omega^{r}_{\log}
is the sheaf of logarithmic de Rham-Witt differentials
[
19
]
.

c.

When
l
is perfect,
L
has equal characteristic
p>0
, and
G
is an RPAU
L
-group, Suzuki
[
40
]
(§4) showed that
H^{q}(L^{\mathrm{ur}},G)
has the structure of an ind-pro-RPAU
l
-group
R^{q}\pi_{L,\mathrm{RP},*}(G)
. Furthermore it belongs to a class of good ind-pro-RPAU groups
\mathcal{W}_{l}
to which duality for RPAU
l
-groups extends, and we have a perfect pairing
R\pi_{L,\mathrm{RP},*}(G)\otimes^{L}R\pi_{L,\mathrm{RP},*}(G^{\vee})\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}[-1]
. This pairing is defined by viewing objects of
\mathcal{W}_{l}
over the
perfect-Artinian étale
or
indrational proétale
sites of
l
defined in
[
36
,
37
]
. If
l
is finite, composing with
R\Gamma(l,-)
yields a perfect pairing of locally compact Hausdorff groups (viewed as condensed groups), recovering approaches of
[
26
]
(III.7 and III.11) and
[
33
]
.

In
(b.)
, if
L
has a
p
-th root of unity then the RPAU structure on
R^{q}\Psi(\mathbb{Z}/p(r))
is given by Kato’s unit filtration on
K^{q}(L)/p\cong H^{q}(L,\mathbb{Z}/p(q))\cong H^{q}(L,\mathbb{Z}/p(r))
, which is bounded with graded objects isomorphic to groups of differentials over
l
. In
(c.)
, the ind-pro-RPAU structure of
R\pi_{L,\mathrm{RP},*}\mathbb{G}_{a,L}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is essentially given by the Tate vector space isomorphism
L\cong l(\!(\pi)\!)\cong\bigoplus_{n<0}lt^{n}\oplus\prod_{n\geq 0}lt^{n}
, where
\mathbb{G}_{a}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is the relative perfection of the additive group.

In this paper we generalize, detail and assemble such results into a statement on higher local fields, varieties over them, and interpret them concretely in terms of étale cohomology. This improves on
[
10
]
by allowing
K
to have
\characteristic(k_{2})=0
instead of
\characteristic(k_{1})=0
.

Main results

We need some notations and definitions to state the results.

For
F
a perfect field, write
F_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
the indrational proétale site of
F
(Definition
39
). Let
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{F}}(F_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})\subseteq D(F_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
be the full subcategory of bounded objects with cohomology representable by "good" ind-pro-RPAU
F
-groups (Definition
35
and Definition
40
). For
X
a scheme and
S_{X}
one of the sites
X_{\mathrm{et}}
(in general) or
X_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
(with
X=\Spec F
), write
D_{\mathrm{fin}}(S_{X})\subseteq D(S_{X})
(resp.
D_{\ell}(S_{X})\subseteq D(S_{X})
with
\ell
a prime) the full subcategory of bounded objects with cohomology representable by finite (resp.
\ell
-primary) étale
X
-groups.

In any symetric monoidal closed category
\mathcal{C}
, given objects
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{C}
we say a morphism
X\otimes Y\to Z
is a perfect pairing if both adjunct maps
X\to\HOM(Y,Z)
and
Y\to\HOM(X,Z)
are isomorphisms.

Let
K=k_{d},\dots,k_{0}
be a sequence of fields such that
k_{0}
is perfect of characteristic
p>0
, and for
i>0
the field
k_{i}
is Henselian discrete valuation with residue field
k_{i-1}
and either
\characteristic k_{i}=0
or
k_{i}
is complete. Let
X
be a proper, smooth, geometrically integral
K
-scheme of relative dimension
e
and
U\subseteq X
a nonempty open. We consider the object
\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(r)=\varinjlim_{m}\mu_{m}^{\otimes r}
of
\Sh(U_{\mathrm{et}})
.

Theorem A
.

(Theorem
79
) Assume
d\geq 2
and
\characteristic k_{2}=0
.

There exist functors
R\Psi_{U/k_{0}},R\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}:D(U_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
with the following properties.

1.

For prime
\ell\neq p
,
R\Psi_{K/k_{0}}
and
R\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}
send
D_{\ell}(K_{\mathrm{et}})
to
D_{\ell}(k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
, and
D_{p}(K_{\mathrm{et}})
to
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k_{0}}}(k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
.

2.

There exists a canonical trace map
\mathrm{tr}:R\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-d-2e]
in
D(k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
.

3.

If
G\in D_{\mathrm{fin}}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
and
G^{\vee}=R\HOM_{U_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d))
, then we have a perfect pairing in
D(k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
:

R\Psi_{U/k_{0}}(G)\otimes^{L}R\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(G^{\vee})\to R\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-d-2e].

4.

If
G\in D_{\mathrm{fin}}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
then
R^{q}\Psi_{U/k_{0}}(G)_{k_{0}^{\mathrm{sep}}}=H^{q}(U_{K^{\mathrm{tur}}},G)
where
K^{\mathrm{tur}}
is the maximal totally unramified extension of
K
(that is, the union of all finite extensions of
K
which induce unramified extensions of
k_{i}
for all
i\geq 1
), and
(-)_{k_{0}^{\mathrm{sep}}}=\varinjlim_{l/k_{0}\text{ finite}}\Gamma(l,-)
denotes the étale stalk at
k_{0}^{\mathrm{sep}}
.

In the case
d=1
(still with
\characteristic K=0
) and
U=X
(but
e=\dim(X)
arbitrary) this theorem is proven in
[
38
]
(Th. 2.8.1) using the étale rational site
k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{rat}}
instead. The essential case of this theorem, with
d=2
,
e=0
and
G=\mu_{p^{n}}^{\otimes r}
, is already present in
[
39
]
(Prop. 6.2.2), albeit in the language of relative sites.

The duality of Theorem
A
is not a purely derived abstraction. If
G\in D_{\ell}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
with
\ell\neq p
, then for
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we have perfect pairing of finite étale
k_{0}
-groups :

R^{q}\Psi_{U/k_{0}}(G)\otimes R^{d+2e-q}\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(G^{\vee})\to R^{d+2e}\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

If
G\in D_{p}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
, then for
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we have so-called Serre dualities of the neutral components and component groups of the cohomology, in the form :

\displaystyle R^{q}\Psi_{U/k_{0}}(G)^{0}

\displaystyle=\EXT^{1}_{k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}(R^{d+2e+1-q}\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(G^{\vee})^{0},\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}),

\displaystyle\pi_{0}(R^{q}\Psi_{U/k_{0}}(G))

\displaystyle=\HOM_{k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}(\pi_{0}(R^{d+2e-q}\Psi_{U/k_{0},c}(G^{\vee})),\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}).

Theorem
A
already gives a concrete statement for the cohomology of
U
when
k_{0}
is algebraically closed. If
k_{0}
is finite,
R\Gamma(k_{0},-)
sends ind-pro-RPAU
k_{0}
-groups to ind-pro-finite groups. This fails if
k_{0}
is only quasi-finite, so in constrast with the case of prime-to-
p
coefficients, a generalization to arbitrary quasi-finite
k_{0}
seems out of reach.

Let
*_{\mathrm{proet}}
be the proétale site of a point (Definition
45
and
[
5
]
). Recall that locally compact Hausdorff topological spaces can be fully faithcully embedded as sheaves of sets over
*_{\mathrm{proet}}
. Write
D_{\mathrm{fin}}(*_{\mathrm{proet}})\subseteq D(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
(resp.
D_{\ell}(*_{\mathrm{proet}})\subseteq D(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
with
\ell
a prime) the subcategory of bounded objects with cohomology representable by finite discrete (resp. and
\ell
-primary) abelian groups. Write
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}(*_{\mathrm{proet}})\subseteq D(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
the subcategory of bounded objects with cohomology representable by "good" ind-pro-finite
p
-groups, which are locally compact Hausdorff spaces (Definition
44
and Definition
45
).

Theorem B
.

(Theorem
81
) Assume
d\geq 2
,
\characteristic k_{2}=0
and
k_{0}
is finite.

There exist functors
R\Psi_{U/*},R\Psi_{U/*,c}:D(U_{\mathrm{et}})\to D(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
with the following properties.

1.

For any prime
\ell\neq p
,
R\Psi_{U/*}
and
R\Psi_{U/*,c}
send
D_{\ell}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
to
D_{\ell}(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
, and
D_{p}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
to
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{p}}(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
. In particular, if
G\in D_{\mathrm{fin}}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
then
R^{q}\Psi_{U/*}(G)
and
R^{q}\Psi_{U/*,c}(G)
are representable by locally compact Hausdorff topological abelian groups for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

2.

There exists a canonical trace map
\mathrm{tr}:R\Psi_{U/*,c}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-d-2e-1]
in
D(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
.

3.

If
G\in D_{\mathrm{fin}}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
and
G^{\vee}=R\HOM_{U_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))
then we have a perfect pairing in
D(k_{0,\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
:

R\Psi_{U/*}(G)\otimes^{L}R\Psi_{U/*,c}(G^{\vee})\to R\Psi_{U/*,c}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}[-d-2e-1].

For
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, this induces perfect Pontryagin dualities of locally compact Hausdorff abelian groups :

R^{q}\Psi_{U/*}(G)\otimes R^{d+2e+1-q}\Psi_{U/*,c}(G^{\vee})\to R^{d+2e+1}\Psi_{U/*,c}(\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\xrightarrow{\mathrm{tr}}\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}.

4.

If
G\in D_{\mathrm{fin}}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
then
R^{q}\Psi_{U/*}(G)(*)=H^{q}(U,G)
and
R^{q}\Psi_{U/*,c}(G)(*)=H^{q}_{c}(U,G)
. In other words,
R^{q}\Psi_{U/*}(G)
and
R^{q}\Psi_{U/*,c}(G)
can be viewed as the groups
H^{q}(U,G)
and
H^{q}_{c}(U,G)
equipped with certain locally compact Hausdorff group topologies.

Corollary C
.

(Corollary
85
)
For
G\in D_{\mathrm{fin}}(U_{\mathrm{et}})
with
G^{\vee}=R\HOM_{U_{\mathrm{et}}}(G,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))
and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
the cup-product pairing
H^{q}(U,G)\otimes H^{d+2e+1-q}_{c}(U,G^{\vee})\to H^{d+2e+1}(U,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}(d+e))\to\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z}
is nondegenerate.

Structure of the paper

The first three parts of the paper focus on recalling, detailing and expanding upon the various formalisms used to obtain the duality results of
[
21
,
40
]
mentioned in the introduction. The fourth focuses on generalizing these duality results, assembling them into points
(1.-3.)
of Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
, and relating them to actual étale cohomology to obtain points
(4.)
of these theorems and Corollary
C
.

In part
1
we give a holistic exposé on commutative relatively perfect group schemes after
[
2
]
(§1, 8) and
[
40
]
(§3). Unipotent such groups, abbreviated RPAU groups, satisfy a duality first outlined in
[
19
]
and expanded upon in
[
21
,
40
]
. We especially detail the dévissage of RPAU groups, the equivalent descriptions of their derived category, and their behaviour with regards to finite field extensions.

In part
2
we recall the formalism of the sites
k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
and
k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
for a perfect field
k
due to Suzuki
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
. We focus on their main features of interest : the class of good ind-pro-RPAU
k
-groups
\mathcal{W}_{k}
can be viewed as sheaves over them in a derived sense, and satisfies a duality as such. We prove
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is stable under extensions, a property used in
[
40
]
to prove duality in the equal characteristic
p
step.

In part
3
we detail some (possibly folklore) comparisons of ind-pro-finite groups, locally compact groups, and condensed groups : the latter fully faithfully contain the former two in a derived sense. The class of good ind-pro-finite groups
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{fin}}
is in the intersection  ; it is stable under Pontryagin duality and extensions of condensed groups, and such extensions respect topology. If
k
is a finite field of characteristic
p>0
,
R\Gamma(k,-)
sends
\mathcal{W}_{k}
to
\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{fin}}
and satisfies a duality - this is the step separating Theorem
A
and Theorem
B
.

Finally in part
4
we prove the main results. We write down the steps corresponding to
(a.)
,
(b.)
and
(c.)
in the introduction, largely citing work of Kato and Suzuki. Extra work is provided in extending the mixed characteristic step to general
p
-primary coefficients, adapting techniques used in
[
2
]
. We deal with prime-to-
p
coefficients with the same languages, adapting many of the same methods though with less complex computations. The higher local duality theorem follows nicely but the interpretation for Galois cohomology still requires some work, to which we dedicate a section.

In an appendix, we recall Kato’s canonical lifting and study its structure.

Acknowledgements.
The author would like to thank Cyril Demarche for his suggestion and continuous support of this project. He would also like to thank Takashi Suzuki for gracefully sharing his insights and comments : several results in this paper are directly based on his advice.

Notations and conventions

All sites considered are defined by pretopologies. Namely for our purposes, a site is a category
S
equipped with a class of families of maps with common target called
covering families
, such that :
S
has fiber products by any map appearing in a covering family  ; any isomorphism in
S
is a one-term covering family  ; covering families are stable under composition and arbitrary base change. See
[
17
]
.

For
S
such a site and
\Lambda
a ring, we write
\mathrm{PSh}(S,\Lambda)
and
\Sh(S,\Lambda)
the categories of presheaves and sheaves of
\Lambda
-modules on
S
. We write
D(S,\Lambda)=D(\Sh(S,\Lambda))
its derived category (and
D^{b}(S,\Lambda)
,
D^{+}(S,\Lambda)
,
D^{-}(S,\Lambda)
the bounded subcategories). We write
\otimes_{\Lambda}
and
\HOM_{S,\Lambda}
the tensor product and inner Hom of
\Sh(S,\Lambda)
. If
\Lambda=\mathbb{Z}
, the ring
\Lambda
is omitted from notations and we write simply
\mathrm{PSh}(S)
,
\Sh(S)
,
D(S)
,
\otimes
and
\HOM_{S}
. On occasion we write
\mathrm{PSh}^{\mathrm{set}}(S)
and
\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(S)
to denote (pre)sheaves of sets on
S
.

A
premorphism
between two sites defined by pretopologies
v:S\to S^{\prime}
is a functor of underlying categories
v^{-1}:S^{\prime}\to S
which sends covering families to covering families, and commutes with base change by morphisms which appear in a covering family. Precomposing by
v^{-1}
defines a functor
v_{*}:\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(S)\to\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(S^{\prime})
which restricts to
v_{*}:\Sh(S,\Lambda)\to\Sh(S^{\prime},\Lambda)
for any ring
\Lambda
, and both admit right adjoints
v^{\mathrm{set}}:\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(S^{\prime})\to\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(S)
and
v^{*}:\Sh(S^{\prime},\Lambda)\to\Sh(S,\Lambda)
. The functor
v^{*}
admits a left derived functor
Lv^{*}:D(S^{\prime},\Lambda)\to D(S,\Lambda)
, which is right adjoint to
Rv_{:}D(S)\to D(S^{\prime})
, and there exists a natural morphism
Rv_{*}(-)\otimes^{L}Rv_{*}(-)\to Rv_{*}(-\otimes^{L}-)
in
D(S^{\prime})
called the cup-product. When
v^{\mathrm{set}}
is exact we say
v
is a
morphism
of site : in that case
v^{*}
is an exact monoidal functor. See
[
37
]
(§2).

For
S
a scheme, we write
S_{\mathrm{Et}}
the
big étale site
of
S
(
[
34
]
Def. 021A and 021B).

In a symetric monoidal closed category
(\mathcal{A},\otimes,\HOM)
, a
pairing
is the data of three objects
X,Y,Z\in\mathcal{A}
and a morphism
X\otimes Y\to Z
. We say it is
perfect
(resp.
nondegenerate
) if the corresponding maps
X\to\HOM(Y,Z)
and
Y\to\HOM(X,Z)
are isomorphisms (resp. monomorphisms).

A
filtration
of an object
G
of an abelian category
\mathcal{A}
is a sequence of subobjects
\dots\leq G_{i+1}\leq G_{i}\leq\dots\leq G_{1}\leq G_{0}=G
in
\mathcal{A}
. Its
i
-th quotient is
G_{i}/G_{i+1}
. It is
finite separated
if
G_{i}=0
for some
i\geq 0
.

1
Relatively perfect group schemes

1.1
Relatively perfect schemes

Fix
S
an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme. For
X/S
, write
X^{(p/S)}
the base change along the absolute Frobenius
F_{S}:S\to S
. This way the absolute Frobenius
F_{X}
factors uniquely through a map
F_{X/S}:X\to X^{(p/S)}
, called the
relative Frobenius of
X
over
S
.

Definition 1
.

We say
X/S
is
relatively perfect over
S
if its relative Frobenius
F_{X/S}
is an isomorphism. The category of relatively perfect
S
-schemes with all
S
-scheme morphisms is written
\mathrm{RPSch}/S
. The
relatively perfect site of

S
is category
\mathrm{RPSch}/S
equipped with the étale topology, which we write
S_{\mathrm{RP}}
.

More precisely,
S_{\mathrm{RP}}
is the full subcategory of
S_{\mathrm{Et}}
of
[
34
]
(Def. 021A and 021B) of objects relatively perfect over
S
, with the étale topology.

Relatively perfect morphisms are stable under composition and base change (
[
19
]
Lem. 1.2), a relatively perfect morphism is formally étale, and étale morphisms of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-schemes are exactly the relatively perfect morphisms of finite presentation (
[
19
]
Lem. 1.3). In particular for
X\in S_{\mathrm{RP}}
,
X_{\mathrm{et}}
is contained in
S_{\mathrm{RP}}
.

Proposition 2
.

(
[
19
]
(Lem. 1.5 and Prop. 1.4)) Assume
F_{S}
is finite locally free.

1.

The functor
X\mapsto X^{(p/S)}
on
\mathrm{Sch}/S
has a right adjoint,
i.e.
the Weil restriction
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}(-)
exists.

2.

The inclusion
\mathrm{RPSch}/S\hookrightarrow\mathrm{Sch}/S
admits a right adjoint, called the
relative perfection over
S
, written
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}/S}
or simply
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}
, given by
X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}^{n}(X)
.

The transitions in
X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}=\varprojlim_{n\geq 0}\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}^{n}(X)
are given by the natural map
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}\to\mathrm{id}
corresponding, under the adjunction between
(-)^{(p/S)}
and
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}
, to the relative Frobenius
\mathrm{id}\to(-)^{(p/S)}
. In other words,
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}^{n+1}(X)\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}^{n}(X)
is the map
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}^{n}(\varepsilon_{X}\circ F_{X/S})
where
\varepsilon_{X}:\mathrm{Res}_{F_{S}}(X^{(p/S)})\to X
is the counit.

The assumption on
F_{S}
is true, for example, if
S
is smooth over a field
k
of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
; or more generally, if
S
admits a covering by affine opens
\Spec R_{i}
such that each
R_{i}
admits a finite
p
-basis. Relative perfection preserves any property preserved by projective limits with affine transitions and Weil restrictions. For instance, the relative perfection of an affine (resp. quasi-compact, quasi-separated)
S
-scheme is again affine (resp. quasi-compact, quasi-separated).

Example 3
.

Let
k
be a reduced
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra which admits a finite
p
-basis
X=(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
. This means the
k
-algebra
k^{(p)}
with underlying ring
k
and structure map given by the absolute Frobenius
F_{k}:k\to k
, admits a free
k
-linear basis given by
\tilde{X}=\{x^{m}:m\in I\}
, where
I=\{(m_{1},\dots,m_{r}),0\leq m_{i}<p\}
and we write
x^{m}=x_{1}^{m_{1}}\cdots x_{r}^{m_{r}}
for
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{r})
. More generally, the
k
-algebra
k^{(p^{n})}
given by the
n
-fold Frobenius
F_{k}^{n}:k\to k
admits a free
k
-linear basis given by :

\tilde{X}_{n}=\{x^{M}:M\in I_{n}\},\qquad I_{n}=\{(M_{1},\dots,M_{r}):0\leq M_{i}<p^{n}\}.

In this situation a
k
-algebra
R
is relatively perfect if and only if the image of
X
is again a
p
-basis of
R
(Lemma
94
.2). For example,
k=[t^{1/p^{\infty}}]=k[t^{1/p^{n}},n\geq 0]
is a relatively perfect
k
-algebra. However unless
k
is perfect,
k[t^{1/p^{\infty}}]
is not the relative perfection of
k[t]
: any element of
k\setminus k^{p}
defines a map
k[t]\to k
which does not factor through
k[t^{1/p^{\infty}}]
, showing failure of the universal property. Rather,
k[t]^{\mathrm{RP}}
is the smallest
k[t]
-algebra closed under taking coordinates in
\tilde{X}
: one can show that
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(k[t])
is the polynomial
k[t]
-algebra
k[t_{M},M\in I_{n}]
whose structure morphism "decomposes
t
in
\tilde{X}_{n}
" :

\displaystyle k[t]

\displaystyle\to k[t_{M}:M\in I_{n}]

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle\mapsto\sum_{M\in I_{n}}t_{M}^{p^{n}}x^{M}

and the transition morphism
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(k[t])\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n+1}(k[t])
is defined by sending
t_{M}
to
\sum_{m\in I}t_{pM+m}^{p}x^{m}
, making the variables in
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n+1}(k[t])
the coordinates of those of
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(k[t])
in the
p
-basis
X
. Then :

k[t]^{\mathrm{RP}}=\left.k\left[t_{(M,n)},(M,n)\in\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}I_{n}\right]\middle/\left\langle t_{(M,n)}-\sum_{m\in I}t_{(pM+m,n+1)}^{p}x^{m},(M,n)\in\bigsqcup_{n\geq 1}I_{n}\right\rangle\right..

In particular
k[t]^{\mathrm{RP}}
is not Noetherian : the ideal
\langle t_{(M,n)},(M,n)\in\bigsqcup_{n}I_{n}\rangle
is not finitely generated.

Definition 4
.

Assume
F_{S}
is finite locally free. We say a scheme
X/S
is
relatively perfectly smooth (resp. relatively perfectly locally of finite presentation) over
S
if, Zariski-locally on
X
and
S
,
X
is isomorphic to the relative perfection of a smooth (resp. finite presentation)
S
-scheme. We write
\mathrm{RPSSch}/S
the category of relatively perfectly smooth
S
-schemes. The
relatively perfectly smooth site
of
S
is the category
\mathrm{RPSSch}/S
equipped with the étale topology. We write it
S_{\mathrm{RPS}}
.

Lemma 5
.

Let
S
be an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
scheme and
T
an
S
-cheme.

1.

For
X
an
S
-scheme,
(X\times_{S}T)^{(p/T)}=X^{(p/S)}\times ST
and under this identification
F_{X\times_{S}T/T}
is the base change of
F_{X/S}
by
T
. In particular if
X
is relatively perfect over
S
then
X\times_{S}T
is relatively perfect over
T
.

2.

Assume
T
is étale over
S
and
X
is an
S
-scheme. Then
(X\times_{S}T)^{\mathrm{RP}/S}=X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}\times_{S}T
.

3.

Assume there exists a map
g:S\to T
which forms a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{T}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{S}^{n}}

for some
n\geq 1
. Then the base change functor
(-)\times_{S}T:\mathrm{RPSch}/S\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{RPSch}/T
is an equivalence of categories with essential inverse given by base change by
g
.

4.

Consider
S
,
T
and
g:S\to T
as in the previous point. Assume furthermore that
F_{S}
,
F_{T}
and
T\to S
are finite locally free. Then for
X\in\mathrm{Sch}/S
there is an isomorphism of
T
-schemes :

X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}\times_{S}T=(\mathrm{Res}_{g}X)^{\mathrm{RP}/T}=\mathrm{Res}_{g}(X^{\mathrm{RP}/S})

and the base change functor restricts to an equivalence on relatively perfectly smooth schemes
(-)\times_{S}T:\mathrm{RPSSch}/S\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathrm{RPSSch}/T
with essential inverse given by base change by
g
.

5.

Assume
S
is the spectrum of a field with finite Frobenius, and
T
is the spectrum of a finite field extension. Then
(-)\times_{S}T
sends
\mathrm{RPSSch}/S
to
\mathrm{RPSSch}/T
.

Point
(1.-2.)
above means the formation of the site
S_{\mathrm{RP}}
is functorial in
S\in\mathrm{Sch}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
. Point
(5.)
says if
S
is the spectrum of a field with finite Frobenius,
S_{\mathrm{RPS}}
is also functorial with respect to finite field extensions. The assumption of
(3.)
holds for instance if
S
is reduced and quasi-compact, and
T\to S
is a finite universal homeomorphism  ; see
[
22
]
(Cor. 20).

Proof of Lemma
5
.

1.
For
U=S,T
write
U^{(p)}
the
U
-scheme
F_{U}:U\to U
. Then
Y^{(p/U)}=Y\times_{U}U^{(p)}
for any
U
-scheme
Y
, and we have
(X\times_{S}T)^{(p/T)}=X^{(p/S)}\times_{S}T
by associativity of fiber products, because the morphisms
T^{(p)}\to S^{(p)},T
and
S^{(p)},T\to S
form a commutative square. Now consider the following commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X\times_{S}T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(X\times_{S}T)^{(p/T)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(p/S)}\times_{S}T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X^{(p/S)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces S}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X\times_{S}T/T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{X/S}}

where the unnamed arrows are projections or structure maps. The composite square is cartesian, and the right-hand square is cartesian. By diagram chasing, it follows that the left-hand square is cartesian.

2.
See
[
2
]
(Prop. 2.5).

3.
If
U
is any
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme, by definition the
n
-fold relative Frobenius
Y\to Y^{(p^{n}/U)}
is a natural isomorphism of
U
-schemes for
Y
relatively perfect over
U
, where
Y^{(p^{n}/U)}
is the base change of
Y
along
F_{U}^{n}:U\to U
. Thus
(-)^{(p^{n}/U)}:\mathrm{RPSch}/U\to\mathrm{RPSch}/U
is naturally isomorphic to identity. We have a commutative diagram of functors :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RPSch}/S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RPSch}/S}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RPSch}/T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{RPSch}/T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{(p^{n}/S)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\times_{S}T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\times_{S}T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{(p^{n}/T)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)\times_{T}S}

where
(-)\times_{T}S
is base change by
g
, and by the above both horizontal arrows are naturally isomorphic to identity. This shows
(-)\times_{S}T
and
(-)\times_{T}S
form an equivalence
\mathrm{RPSch}/S\cong\mathrm{RPSch}/T
.

4.
The morphism
f:T\to S
is quasi-compact and flat by assumption, and surjective because
f\circ g=F_{S}^{n}
is surjective. The flatness of
F_{T}
now implies that of
g
by
[
34
]
(Lem. 02L2). By
[
34
]
(Lem. 0560 and 00F4 (4)),
g
is also finite and of finite presentation because
F_{S}^{n}
and
f
are. Thus in this context,
g:S\to T
is also finite locally free and
f
and
g
play symetric roles. In particular, the functor
(-)\times_{T}S:\mathrm{Sch}/T\to\mathrm{Sch}/S
admits a right adjoint
\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}
. For
X
an
S
-scheme and
Y\in\mathrm{RPSch}/T
, we have :

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathrm{RPSch}/T}(Y,X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}\times_{S}T)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{RPSch}/S}(Y\times_{T}S,X^{\mathrm{RP}/S})

by equivalence,

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{Sch}/S}(Y\times_{T}S,X)

\displaystyle\text{by definition of }(-)^{\mathrm{RP}/S},

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{Sch}/T}(Y,\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}X)

\displaystyle\text{by definition of }\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}

\displaystyle=\Hom_{\mathrm{RPSch}/T}(Y,(\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}X)^{\mathrm{RP}/T})

\displaystyle\text{by definition of }(-)^{\mathrm{RP}/T}.

thus
X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}\times_{S}T=(\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}X)^{\mathrm{RP}/T}
. We also have
Y\times_{S}T=\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}(Y)
for any
Y\in\mathrm{RPSch}/S
, by uniqueness of the right adjoint of
(-)\times_{T}S
.

Because
f
and
g
play symetric roles, it remains only to show
(-)\times_{S}T
sends
\mathrm{RPSSch}/S
into
\mathrm{RPSSch}/T
. By compatibility of base change with Zariski coverings, we need to see
X^{\mathrm{RP}}\times_{S}T
is relatively perfectly smooth over
T
if
X
is
S
-smooth. This follows easily from the formula
X^{\mathrm{RP}/S}\times_{S}T=(\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}X)^{\mathrm{RP}/T}
and the fact that the Weil restriction
\mathrm{Res}_{S/T}
sends
S
-smooth schemes to
T
-smooth ones.

5.
Write
S=\Spec k
and
T=\Spec l
. Consider
X\in\mathrm{RPSSch}/S
. After taking a suitable Zariski covering of
X
, we can assume
X=X_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
for some
S
-smooth
X_{0}
. By
[
34
]
(Lem. 030K) and the associativity of fiber products, we reduce to the cases where
l/k
is either finite separable or finite purely inseparable. The separable case is by
(2.)
, since
X_{0}\times_{k}l
is
l
-smooth. In the purely inseparable case, there exists some
n\geq 1
such that
l^{p^{n}}
is contained in
k
by
[
34
]
(Lem. 09HI) (for instance, we can take
p^{n}=[l:k]
). Then the
n
-fold Frobenius
F_{l}^{n}:l\to l
takes values in
k
, giving a factorization :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces k}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{k}^{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{l}^{n}}

and we conclude that
(-)\times_{k}l
sends
\mathrm{RPSSch}/k
to
\mathrm{RPSSch}/l
by
(4.)
.
∎

As seen in Example
3
, the relative perfection of a Noetherian scheme is not necessarily Noetherian. Nevertheless, over nice
S
, relatively perfectly smooth quasi-compact
S
-schemes behave as Noetherian with respect to relatively perfect subschemes.

Proposition 6
.

We have the following.

1.

Assume
S=\Spec k
where
k
is a reduced
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra which admits a
p
-basis. Consider
\tilde{X}
a smooth
k
-scheme and
X=\tilde{X}^{\mathrm{RP}}
.

(a)

The map
X\to\tilde{X}
is faithfully flat.

(b)

If
Y\subseteq X
is a closed subscheme relatively perfect over
S
, then it is the relative perfection of a reduced closed subscheme
\tilde{Y}\subseteq\tilde{X}
.

(c)

The assignment
Y\mapsto\tilde{Y}
is functorial, meaning if
Y\subseteq Y^{\prime}\subseteq X
are closed immersions of relatively perfect
S
-schemes, then we have closed immersions of reduced
S
-schemes
\tilde{Y}\subseteq\tilde{Y}^{\prime}\subseteq\tilde{X}
.

2.

Assume
S
is Noetherian, reduced and admits an affine open cover
S=\bigcup_{i}\Spec R_{i}
such that each
R_{i}
admits
p
-bases. Consider a sequence of closed immersions of
S
-schemes :

X_{0}\supseteq X_{1}\supseteq X_{2}\supseteq\dots\supseteq X_{n}\supseteq\dots

Assume
X_{n}
is relatively perfect over
S
, for all
n\geq 0
, and
X_{0}
is quasi-compact and relatively perfectly smooth over
S
. Then the sequence is stationary, i.e.
X_{m}=X_{n_{0}}
for some
n_{0}\geq 0
and all
m\geq n_{0}
.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

1.a
We adapt
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.13). The relative Frobenius
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n})\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n})^{(p)}
is always a universal homeomorphism (hence surjective), and is finite flat because
F_{k}
is. The counit
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n})^{(p)}\to\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n}
is a surjective map of finite free
k
-modules, hence is faithfully flat, given after any choice of
p
-basis
(x_{1},\dots,x_{r})
of
k
as the map :

\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n})^{(p)}\cong((\mathbb{A}_{k}^{p^{r}})^{n})^{(p)}\to\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n};\qquad(t_{m})_{m\in I}\mapsto\sum_{m\in I}t_{m}^{p}x^{m}

with the notations of Example
3
. Iterating (since the above applies to
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{m}(\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k})\cong\mathbb{A}^{np^{mr}}_{k}
), each map
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{m}(\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k})\to\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n}
is faithfully flat, hence the limit
(\mathbb{A}^{n}_{k})^{\mathrm{RP}}\to\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n}
is faithfully flat by
[
34
]
(Lem. 05UU).

By
[
34
]
(Lem. 054L), we can choose a Zariski covering
\tilde{X}=\bigcup_{i}\tilde{U}_{i}
, some
n_{i}\geq 0
and étale maps
\tilde{U}_{i}\to\mathbb{A}^{n_{i}}_{k}
. By Lemma
5
.2,
X=\bigcup_{i}U_{i}
is a Zariski covering with
U_{i}=\tilde{U}_{i}^{\mathrm{RP}}=\tilde{U}_{i}\times_{\tilde{X}}X
and we have a cartesian square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n_{i}})^{\mathrm{RP}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{U}_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{A}_{k}^{n_{i}}}

where the vertical map on the right is faithfully flat by the previous case, so
U_{i}\to\tilde{U}_{i}
is faithfully flat. Hence
X\to\tilde{X}
is surjective, and flat Zariski-locally on
\tilde{X}
, hence globally so.

1.b
We shall adapt (and add detail to)
[
7
]
(Th. 3.1.6.(2)). Consider
\tilde{Y}\subseteq\tilde{X}
the scheme-theoretic image of the composite
Y\to X\to\tilde{X}
. Then
\tilde{Y}
is a reduced closed subscheme of
\tilde{X}
by
[
34
]
(Lem. 01R8.(2) and 056B, using Lemma
94
.1). We have a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}^{\mathrm{RP}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}\times_{\tilde{X}}X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{X}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces v}

where all horizontal maps are all closed immersions -
u_{3}\circ u_{2}\circ u_{1}
and
v
by construction,
u_{3}
by base change of
v
,
u_{3}\circ u_{2}
by relative perfection of
v
(
[
2
]
Rem. 2.4.(3)), hence
u_{2}
and
u_{1}
by simplification.

By
[
34
]
(Lem. 081I) and flatness of
X\to\tilde{X}
, the scheme
\tilde{Y}\times_{\tilde{X}}X\subseteq X
is the scheme-theoretic image of
Y\times_{\tilde{X}}X\to X
. In particular the map
Y\times_{\tilde{X}}X\to\tilde{Y}\times_{\tilde{X}}X
is dominant. But it also factors through the projection
Y\times_{\tilde{X}}X\to Y
, which is faithfully flat by
(1.a)
and base change. Thus
Y\to\tilde{Y}\times_{\tilde{X}}X
is both dominant and a closed immersion, so it is bijective on topological spaces. In particular
Y\to\tilde{Y}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is a bijective closed immersion of reduced schemes, hence an isomorphism.

1.c.
As seen in
(1.b)
, we can take
\tilde{Y}
to be the scheme-theoretic image of
Y
in
\tilde{X}
. The formation of this image is functorial by
[
34
]
(Lem. 01R9).

2.
By taking finite Zariski coverings of
S
and
X_{0}
, we can assume
S=\Spec k
with
k
a Noetherian, reduced
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra equipped with a
p
-basis and
X_{0}=\tilde{X}_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
for some affine smooth
S
-scheme
X_{0}
. By
(1.c)
, the sequence of closed immersions
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is the relative perfection of a sequence of closed immersions :

\tilde{X}_{0}\supseteq\tilde{X}_{1}\supseteq X_{2}\supseteq\dots\supseteq\tilde{X}_{n}\supseteq\dots

which must be stationary because
\tilde{X}_{0}
is Noetherian. Hence
\{X_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
is stationary.
∎

The following definition will be used in section
4.2
.

Definition 7
.

Let
K
be a Henselian discrete valuation field of mixed characteristic
(0,p)
, with residue field
k
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
. Define
\mathrm{RPSSch}/\mathcal{O}_{K}
as the category of schemes over the ring of integers
\mathcal{O}_{K}
which are
\mathcal{O}_{K}
-flat and whose special fiber belongs to
\mathrm{RPSSch}/k
. The
relatively perfectly smooth site
of
\Spec\mathcal{O}_{K}
is the category
\mathrm{RPSSch}/\mathcal{O}_{K}
equipped with the étale topology. We write it
\mathcal{O}_{K,\mathrm{RPS}}
.

In the rest of the paper, we will almost exclusively use the relatively perfect(ly smooth) site for
S
the spectrum of a field. For more properties of relatively perfect schemes over a Dedekind scheme, see
[
27
]
.

Remark 8
.

For
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
, define
S_{\tau,\mathrm{qcqs}}
(resp.
S_{\tau,\mathrm{aff}}
) as the category of objects of
S_{\tau}
quasi-compact quasi-separated (resp. affine) over
S
, with the étale topology. Most of this part
1
holds verbatim if we replace
S_{\tau}
with
S_{\tau,\mathrm{qcqs}}
(resp.
S_{\tau,\mathrm{aff}}
), because relative perfection preserves affine, quasi-compact or quasi-separated morphisms, and the RPA (resp. RPAU) group schemes considered thereafter are quasicompact (resp. affine). One checks that
[
2
]
§8 and
[
40
]
§3 still hold for these sites when restricting the discussion to only quasicompact quasiseparated (resp. affine) group schemes.

1.2
RPAU group schemes

From now on
S=\Spec k
is the spectrum of a field
k
of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
. Recall that a commutative group scheme
G/k
is
unipotent
if it is affine and every nontrivial closed subgroup
H\leq G
admits a nontrivial
k
-group morphism to the additive group
H\to\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
.

Lemma 9
.

Let
G/k
be a commutative group scheme. The following are equivalent :

1.

G
is (resp. unipotent) quasi-compact and relatively perfectly locally of finite presentation  ;

2.

G
is (resp. unipotent) quasi-compact and relatively perfectly smooth over
k
;

3.

G
is the relative perfection of a (resp. unipotent) quasi-compact, smooth
k
-group.

We then say
G
is
relatively perfectly algebraic (resp. unipotent) over
k
(abbreviated RPA, resp. RPAU).

Over perfect
k
, the perfection of an algebraic group is sometimes called
quasi-algebraic
. Thus RPA groups, where perfection is replaced with relative perfection, generalize these to imperfect
k
.

Proof of Lemma
9
.

See Prop. 8.7 of
[
2
]
and the discussion preceding it, for the non-unipotent version. A commutative unipotent group satisfying those conditions is the same as the relative perfection of a unipotent, commutative, quasi-compact, smooth
k
-group by
[
40
]
(Prop. 3.1).
∎

Definition 10
.

We write
\Alg(k)
(resp.
\Alg_{u}(k)
) the category of (resp. unipotent) commutative, quasi-compact smooth
k
-group schemes with
k
-group scheme morphisms. We write
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
(resp.
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}_{u}(k)
) the category of commutative RPA (resp. RPAU)
k
-group schemes with
k
-group scheme morphisms. For
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
, we write
\Sh_{a}(k_{\tau})
(resp.
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
) the full subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\tau})
of sheaves representable by objects of
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
(resp.
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
).

If instead we consider all finite presentation commutative
k
-groups, the resulting category would be an abelian subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{fppf}})
(
[
9
]

\mathrm{VI}_{\mathrm{A}}
, Th. 3.2). By considering only smooth
k
-groups, we ensure the exact structure étale-local (
i.e.

\Alg(k)
is an exact subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{Et}})
;
[
1
]
17.16.3 (ii)), but
\Alg(k)
is not abelian. Thus it is remarkable that
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
are abelian categories of étale sheaves, in the sense of Proposition
12
.

Lemma 11
.

Let
u:\mathcal{C}_{2}\to\mathcal{C}_{1}
be a premorphism of sites. Assume
\mathcal{C}_{1}
and
\mathcal{C}_{2}
admit finite products and
u^{-1}
preserves those products. Let
\mathcal{A}_{i}
be the category of commutative group objects of
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and
Y_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\Sh(\mathcal{C}_{i})
be the Yoneda functor. Then
u^{*}\circ Y_{1}=Y_{2}\circ u^{-1}
as functors
\mathcal{A}_{1}\to\Sh(\mathcal{C}_{2})
.

Proof of Lemma
11
.

The same statement about the Yoneda functors
\mathcal{C}_{i}\to\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(\mathcal{C}_{i})
and
u^{\mathrm{set}}
is true with no particular assumption on
u
, however in that case
u^{-1}
does not preserve group objects and does not restrict to a functor
\mathcal{A}_{1}\to\mathcal{A}_{2}
. For
G\in\mathcal{A}_{1}
and
F\in\Sh(\mathcal{C}_{2})
we have :

\Hom_{\Sh(\mathcal{C}_{2})}(u^{*}Y_{1}(G),F)=\Hom_{\Sh(\mathcal{C}_{1})}(Y_{1}(G),u_{*}F)=(u_{*}F)(G)=F(u^{-1}G)=\Hom_{\Sh(\mathcal{C}_{2})}(Y_{2}(u^{-1}G),F)

where the first equality is by definition of
u^{*}
, the second by Yoneda’s lemma, the third by definition of
u_{*}
, the fourth by Yoneda’s lemma again. Hence
u^{*}Y_{1}(G)=Y_{2}(u^{-1}G)
canonically.
∎

Proposition 12
.

For
k
a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
, the following holds.

1.

\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is abelian and Noetherian, and
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is an abelian subcategory stable under extensions.

2.

For
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
,
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
and
\Sh_{a}(k_{\tau})
are full subcategories of
\Sh(k_{\tau})
stable under extensions.

3.

The Yoneda functor, and pushforward and pullback of the premorphism
k_{\mathrm{RP}}\to k_{\mathrm{RPS}}
given by identity, are equivalences
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\cong\Sh_{a}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\cong\Sh_{a}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
and
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}_{u}(k)\cong\Sh_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\cong\Sh_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
.

Proof of Proposition
12
.

Write
v:k_{\mathrm{RP}}\to k_{\mathrm{RPS}}
the premorphism given by identity. The Yoneda functor
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}})(k)\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
is fully faithful by Yoneda’s lemma and Lemma
9
, and essentially surjective by definition. The rest of the proposition for RPA groups, except Noetherianity and properties of
v^{*}
, is
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.12 and 8.17). Any monomorphism in
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is a kernel, hence a closed immersion, so the Noetherian property follows from Proposition
6
.2. The category
k_{\mathrm{RPS}}
has finite products and
v^{-1}
preserves them, so
v^{*}
sends
\Sh_{a}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
to
\Sh_{a}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
by Lemma
11
. The restriction
v^{*}:\Sh_{a}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})\to\Sh_{a}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
is right adjoint to
v_{*}
, hence its essential inverse. Clearly
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is closed under kernels, cokernels and extensions in
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, and
v^{*}
sends
\Sh_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
to
\Sh_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
, specializing the results to RPAU groups.
∎

1.3
Dévissage for RPAU groups

Let
k
be again a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
.

Proposition 13
.

Consider the relative perfection functor
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}
.

1.

(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}:\Alg(k)\to\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is an exact functor, and
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}:\mathrm{Sch}/k\to\mathrm{RPSch}/k
preserves limits.

2.

(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}:\Alg(k)\to\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is full and essentially surjective.

3.

If
A_{0}\in\Alg(k)
and
0\to A_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to B\to C\to 0
is an exact sequence in
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, then it is the relative perfection of an exact sequence
0\to A_{0}\to B_{0}\to C_{0}\to 0
in
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

4.

For
G\in\Alg(k)
we have canonical isomorphisms
\pi_{0}(G^{\mathrm{RP}})\cong\pi_{0}(G)
and
(G^{0})^{\mathrm{RP}}\cong(G^{\mathrm{RP}})^{0}
.

5.

All of the above holds again if we replace
\Alg(k)
and
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
by
\Alg_{u}(k)
and
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

Exactness of
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}
on the nonabelian category
\Alg(k)
means if
0\to N\to G\to H\to 0
is an exact sequence in
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{Et}})
, with
N,G,H\in\Alg(k)
, then
0\to N^{\mathrm{RP}}\to G^{\mathrm{RP}}\to H^{\mathrm{RP}}\to 0
is exact in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
for
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
. If we consider all finite presentation commutative groups, then
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}
is still left exact as a right adjoint, but the relative perfection of a non-smooth fppf map may not be surjective (
[
2
]
Ex. 8.11).

Proof of Proposition
13
.

1.
This functor preserves limits (and in particular is left exact) as right adjoint to the inclusion. It is right exact because it preserves smooth surjections by
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.8).

2.
Essential surjectivity is by definition of RPA
k
-groups,
i.e.
Proposition
9
. Consider
G,H\in\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
G_{0},H_{0}\in\Alg(k)
such that
G=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
and
H=H_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. Then
G=\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}
where
G_{n}=\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(G_{0})
. By definition of the relative perfection and
[
34
]
(Prop. 01ZC) :

\Hom_{\mathrm{RPSch}/k}(G,H)=\Hom_{\mathrm{Sch}/k}(G,H_{0})=\varinjlim_{n}\Hom_{\mathrm{Sch}/k}(G_{n},H_{0})

and these identifications restrict to the subsets of morphisms of group objects. By Lemma
5
.4 (with
S=T=\Spec k
and
g=F_{k}^{n}
), we have
G_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}}=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}\times_{k,F_{k}^{n}}\Spec k
, and applying the relative Frobenius we get
G_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}}=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}=G
. Thus any morphism
G\to H
is the relative perfection of a morphism
G_{n}\to H_{0}
.

It only remains to show
G_{n}\in\Alg(k)
, which reduces to showing
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(G_{0})\in\Alg(k)
for
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
, after an induction on
n
. The Weil restriction of a quasi-compact (resp. smooth)
k
-group is again quasi-compact (resp. smooth), which concludes.

3.
First we show any short exact sequence
(E):0\to A\to B\to C\to 0
in
\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is the image of a short exact sequence
0\to A_{1}\to B_{1}\to C_{1}\to 0
in
\Alg(k)
without prescribing
A_{1}
.

By
(2.)
, the morphism
f:B\to C
is the relative perfection of a morphism
f_{1}:B_{1}\to\tilde{C}_{1}
in
\Alg(k)
. Its kernel
\tilde{A}_{1}
is a finite type
k
-group but not necessarily smooth  ; the maximal smooth subgroup
A_{1}=(\tilde{A}_{1})^{\sharp}
(
[
2
]
Def. 8.3) thus belongs to
\Alg(k)
. By
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.4) and left exactness of relative perfection, we have
A_{1}^{\mathrm{RP}}=(\tilde{A}_{1})^{\mathrm{RP}}=\ker(f)
. Set
C_{1}=B_{1}/A_{1}
. Then
C_{1}
belongs to
\Alg(k)
as quotient of a smooth group
[
6
]
(Ex. A.1.12). By
(1.)
we have an exact sequence
0\to A_{1}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to B_{1}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to C_{1}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to 0
. It follows that
C_{1}^{\mathrm{RP}}=\coker(A\to B)=\tilde{C}_{1}^{\mathrm{RP}}
, so
(E)
is the relative perfection of the exact sequence
0\to A_{1}\to B_{1}\to C_{1}\to 0
.

Now consider some fixed
A_{0}\in\Alg(k)
such that
A=A_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. Using the previous construction, we have
A=\varprojlim_{n}A_{1,n}
where
A_{1,n}=\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(A_{1})
, so by
[
34
]
(Prop. 01ZC) the natural map
A\to A_{0}
factors as
A\to A_{1,n}\to A_{0}
for some
n
. Similarly write
B_{A,n}=\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(B_{1})
and
C_{1,n}=\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}(C_{1})
and consider the commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{1,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{1,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1,n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

where
B_{0}
is defined as the pushout of
B_{1,n}
and
A_{0}
, and
C_{0}=C_{1,n}
. The Weil restriction of a smooth quasi-compact group is again smooth quasi-compact hence
C_{0}
and (as quotient of the smooth quasi-compact group
B_{A,n}\oplus A_{0}
)
B_{0}
belong to
\Alg(k)
. By the same proof as
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.8) (applied to the functor
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}^{n}:\Alg(k)\to\Alg(k)
instead of
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}:\Alg(k)\to\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
), the first row in the above diagram is exact. By pushout the second row is also exact. Since
A=A_{1,n}^{\mathrm{RP}}=A_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
and
C=C_{1,n}^{\mathrm{RP}}=C_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
, by
(1.)
the map
B=B_{1,n}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to B_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is an isomorphism. Thus
0\to A_{0}\to B_{0}\to C_{0}\to 0
has relative perfection
(E)
.

4.
By
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.13), the canonical morphism
G^{\mathrm{RP}}\to G
induces a bijection between irreducible components, which immediately gives
\pi_{0}(G^{\mathrm{RP}})\cong\pi_{0}(G)
, thus by left exactness
(G^{0})^{\mathrm{RP}}\cong(G^{\mathrm{RP}})^{0}
.

5.
Point
(1.)
and
(4.)
clearly specialize to the exact subcategory
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\subseteq\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

For
(2.)
, everything easily adapts except that
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(G_{0})
is unipotent for
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
. If
G_{0}
is
p
-torsion, by
[
6
]
(Cor. B.1.13) and left exactness of Weil restriction we have a closed immersion of groups
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(G_{0})\subseteq\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{r})
for some
r\geq 0
, where
\mathrm{Res}_{k}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{r})\cong\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{[k:k^{p}]r}
is unipotent by Example
3
, hence
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(G_{0})
is unipotent. In general we have exact sequences for
i\geq 0
:

0\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i+1}G_{0})\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0})\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0}/p^{i+1}G_{0}).

For large
i\geq 0
,
p^{i}G_{0}=0
by Lemma
16
.1 below (whose proof does not use the fullness of
\Alg_{u}(k)\to\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
), and
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0})
is unipotent. Also
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0}/p^{i+1}G_{0})
is unipotent by the
p
-torsion case, thus the closed subgroup
\im(\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0})\to\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0}/p^{i+1}G_{0}))
is unipotent. We conclude that
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(p^{i}G_{0})
is unipotent for all
i\geq 0
, by extensions of unipotent groups, so
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}(G_{0})\in\Alg_{u}(k)
.

For
(3.)
, the proof is identical using the above fact that
\mathrm{Res}_{F_{k}}
sends
\Alg_{u}(k)
to itself.
∎

Definition 14
.

A group
G\in\Alg_{u}(k)
(resp.
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
) is called
(resp. relatively perfect) split
if it admits a finite separated filtration with nontrivial quotients
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
(resp.
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
). It is called
(resp. relatively perfect) wound
if all morphisms
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}\to G
(resp.
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to G
) are trivial.

We also call RPAU
k
-groups
split
or
wound
when it is clear from context that they belong to
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

Lemma 15
.

Consider
G,H\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

1.

If
G
and
H
are respectively split and wound, then any morphism
G\to H
is trivial.

2.

The group
G
is split (resp. wound) if and only if
G=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
with
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
split (resp. wound).

3.

There exists a unique exact sequence
0\to G_{s}\to G\to G_{w}\to 0
where
G_{s}
(resp.
G_{w}
) is relatively perfect split (resp. wound) unipotent. Any morphism
H\to G
with
H
split (resp.
G\to H
with
H
wound) factors uniquely through
G_{s}
(resp.
G_{w}
).

4.

If
G
is split and
G\to H
is a surjection in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
then
H
is split. If
G
is wound and
H\to G
is an injection in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
then
H
is wound.

If
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is split (resp. finite étale) then it is connected (resp. wound). If
k
is perfect then the converses are true, by Lemma
15
.1 and Proposition
13
.

Proof of Lemma
15
.

1.
Consider a finite separated filtration
\dots\leq G_{2}\leq G_{1}\leq G_{0}=G
with quotients
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
for
i\geq 0
. The restriction map
\Hom_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G_{i},H)\to\Hom_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G_{i+1},H)
has kernel
\Hom_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}},H)=0
because
H
is wound. Thus
\Hom_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,H)\to\Hom_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G_{i},H)
is injective, but
G_{i}=0
for sufficiently large
i
, so
\Hom_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,H)=0
, proving the claim.

2.
By Proposition
13
.1, the relative perfection of a split group is split. If
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
is wound unipotent then
\Hom(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}},G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}})=\Hom_{k}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}},G_{0})=\varinjlim_{n}\Hom_{k}(\mathrm{Res}^{n}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}),G_{0})
where all Hom-sets are of
k
-group schemes, by
[
2
]
(Prop. 8.10). Since as a
k
-group
\mathrm{Res}^{n}_{F_{k}}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{[k:k^{p}]^{n}}
by Example
3
, we conclude that
G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is relatively perfect wound unipotent.

Now take any
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
such that
G=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
, and consider
0\to G_{0,s}\to G_{0}\to G_{0,w}\to 0
an exact sequence with split unipotent
G_{0,s}
and wound unipotent
G_{w,0}
: such a sequence exists by
[
6
]
(Th. B.3.4). By Proposition
13
.1, we have an exact sequence in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
:

0\to G_{0,s}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to G\to G_{0,w}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to 0

where we have just seen that
G_{0,s}^{\mathrm{RP}}
(resp.
G_{0,w}^{\mathrm{RP}}
) was relatively perfect split (resp. wound) unipotent. If
G
is relatively perfect wound unipotent, then
G_{0,s}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to G
is trivial and
G=G_{0,w}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. If
G
is relatively perfect split unipotent, then
G\to G_{0,w}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is trivial and
G=G_{0,s}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. This concludes.

3.
The existence is given by the analogous result for
\Alg_{u}(k)

[
6
]
(Th. B.3.4), and Proposition
13
.1. To see uniqueness, it suffices to prove the universal properties, but these are a consequence of
(1.)
as soon as the exact sequence
0\to G_{s}\to G\to G_{w}\to 0
with split
G_{s}
and wound
G_{w}
is given.

4.
The wound statement is clear from the definition. If
G
is split then the composite
G\to H\to H_{w}
is
0
by
(1.)
, so
G\to H
is surjective and factors as
G\to H_{s}\to H
. This implies
H_{s}=H
.
∎

Proposition 16
.

Let
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

1.

The group
G
is
p^{r}
-torsion for some
r\geq 0
, and there exists a finite separated filtration of
G
in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
with
p
-torsion quotients.

2.

If
G
is
p
-torsion connected, there is an exact sequence
0\to G\to(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}\to\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to 0
with
r\geq 1
.

3.

If
G
is
p
-torsion, there exists a monomorphism
G\to(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}
with
r\geq 1
.

Proof of Proposition
16
.

1.
By Lemma
9
,
G=\tilde{G}_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
for some unipotent commutative finite presentation
k
-group
\tilde{G}_{0}
. Because
\tilde{G}_{0}
is unipotent, we can construct a decreasing sequence of subgroups
\tilde{G}_{i}
of
\tilde{G}_{0}
as follows : for
i\geq 0
, either
\tilde{G}_{i}
is trivial, or it admits a nontrivial map
\varphi_{i}:\tilde{G}_{i}\to\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
and we set
\tilde{G}_{i+1}=\ker(\varphi_{i})
. In particular this sequence decreases strictly, either indefinitely or until it stabilizes at
0
. Because
\tilde{G}_{0}
is Noetherian, we have
\tilde{G}_{r}=0
for some
r\geq 0
. Thus
\tilde{G}_{0}
is
p^{r}
-torsion as a successive extension of
r
subgroups of
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
, and
G
is
p^{r}
-torsion by additivity of
(-)^{\mathrm{RP}}
.

Then the subgroups
G_{i}=\im(p^{i}:G\to G)
define a decreasing filtration of
G
in the abelian category
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, with
p
-torsion quotients, and
G_{i}=0
for large enough
i
because
G
is finite
p
-primary exponent.

2.
If
G=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
with
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
, then by
[
6
]
(Cor. B.1.13) there is a nonzero morphism
f:\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{r}\to\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
with kernel
G_{0}
. It is surjective because
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
is its only own smooth connected nontrivial split subgroup. We conclude for
G
by Proposition
13
.1.

3.
If
G=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
with
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
, then
G_{0}
is affine so it embeds in some
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{r}
by
[
6
]
(Lem. B.1.10).
∎

Proposition 17
.

Let
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
. Then
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
is the smallest full abelian subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\tau})
closed under extensions which contains
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
.

Proof of Proposition
17
.

By Proposition
12
,
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
is a full abelian subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\tau})
closed under extensions, and it clearly contains
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. Consider
\mathcal{A}\subseteq\Sh(k_{\tau})
a full abelian subcategory closed under extensions containing
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. By Proposition
16
.2, it contains all
p
-torsion connected objects as well. If
G\in\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
is
p
-torsion, it fits in an exact sequence
0\to G\to(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}\to H\to 0
for some
H\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
by Proposition
16
.3, and
H
is automatically
p
-torsion and connected, hence in
\mathcal{A}
, as the image of the
p
-torsion connected group
(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}
. By extension,
\mathcal{A}
contains all
p
-torsion objects of
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
. In turn it contains all of
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
by Proposition
16
.1, which concludes.
∎

1.4
Derived category of RPAU groups

The goal of this section is to prove a derived version of Proposition
9
. Its content leads up to Proposition
22
and finally Proposition
23
. To prove Proposition
22
, we reproduce a proof communicated by Suzuki. This proof takes inspiration from
[
32
]
(§8.8, Prop. 8) but uses
\mathrm{I}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
instead of
\mathrm{P}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

In this section let
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
be fixed. We write
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
the full subcategory of
p
-torsion objects of
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. From Proposition
12
, it follows that
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
is an abelian subcategory of
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, equivalent to the full subcategory
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
of
p
-torsion objects of
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
.

Lemma 18
.

The additive group
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is injective in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
.

Proof of Lemma
18
.

Consider
f:\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}\to G
a monomorphism in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
. By Proposition
13
.3, this is the image of a closed immersion
f_{0}:\mathbb{G}_{a,k}\to G_{0}
with
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
. By
[
6
]
(Cor. B.1.12) this
f_{0}
splits, so
f
is a split monomorphism.
∎

Proposition 19
.

The Yoneda functor
Y:\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}},\mathbb{Z}/p)
is exact and its derived functor
RY:D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p))\to D^{b}(\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}},\mathbb{Z}/p))
is fully faithful.

Proof of Proposition
19
.

The exactness of
Y
is by Proposition
12
. We need to show equalities :

\Ext^{q}_{\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)}(G,H)=\Ext^{q}_{k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p}(Y(G),Y(H))

for
q\geq 0
and
G,H\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
. By Proposition
17
, we can assume
G=H=\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
. For
q=0
, the equality Proposition
12
. For
q\geq 1
, the right-hand side vanishes by
[
19
]
(Prop. 2.1), and the left-hand side vanishes by Lemma
18
, which concludes.
∎

Proposition 20
.

Any injective object of
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is
p
-divisible.

Proof of Proposition
20
.

Let
I\in\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
be injective. Consider
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

Consider the Cohen ring
C_{n}=\mathcal{J}_{n}^{B}
of
[
2
]
(Def. 7.4) for
n\geq 0
, viewed as a presheaf of rings over
\mathrm{Sch}/k
(by convention,
C_{0}
is trivial). This defines a commutative
k
-group by
[
2
]
(Prop. 7.6), and by
[
30
]
(Prop. 2.9) we have exact sequences :

0\to C_{n}\to C_{n+1}\to\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{(p/k)}\to 0

so
C_{n}
is unipotent and finite presentation for all
n\geq 0
by induction, and
C_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. For
0\leq m\leq n
we have a closed immersion
j_{m,n}:C_{m}\to C_{n}
, and an identification of subgroups
C_{m}(X)=p^{n-m}C_{n}(X)
for
X\in\mathrm{RPSch}/k
. Hence
C_{m}^{\mathrm{RP}}=p^{n-m}C_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}}
and
C_{\infty}^{\mathrm{RP}}=\varinjlim_{n}C_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}}
is
p
-divisible in
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

Write
G=G_{0}^{\mathrm{RP}}
with
G_{0}\in\Alg_{u}(k)
. By
[
30
]
(Prop. 4.7), there exists a closed immersion
G_{0}\to C_{n}^{r}
for some
n,r\geq 0
. By left exactness of relative perfection, we have a monomorphism
G\to(C_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}
in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, hence a monomorphism
G\to(C_{\infty}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}
in
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Then any morphism
G\to I
factors as
G\to(C_{\infty}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}\to I
by injectivity. In particular,
G\to I
factors as
G\to pI\to I
by
p
-divisibility of
(C_{\infty}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{r}
. It follows that the natural map :

\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,pI)\to\Hom_{\mathrm{I}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,I)

is surjective for
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Since injectivity is clear, and Hom functors preserve limits, it follows that the inclusion
pI\to I
induces an isomorphism :

\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,pI)\to\Hom_{\mathrm{I}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,I)

for any
G\in\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Thus
pI=I
by Yoneda’s lemma, which concludes.
∎

To work with
\mathrm{PAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
instead, as in
[
32
]
(§8.8, Prop. 8), we would need every projective objects in
\mathrm{PAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
to be
p
-torsion free. With a dual approach, we would want every
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
to be a quotient of some
Q_{n}^{r}
, where
Q_{n}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
fits in a codirected sequence
\{Q_{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
with surjective transitions such that
\ker(Q_{n}\to Q_{m})=Q_{n}[p^{n-m}]
for
0\leq m\leq n
. Many such families are known (the Witt rings
\{W_{n}\}
and more generally de Rham-Witt differentials
\{W_{n}\Omega^{r}\}
for
0\leq r\leq d
, the Cohen rings
\{C_{n}^{\mathrm{RP}}\}
as above, and logarithmic differentials
\{\nu_{n}(r)\}
for
0\leq r\leq d
), however it is not known that all
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is a quotient of a finite sum of them (note that families of wound groups must be included, since no nonzero wound group can be a quotient of a split group).

Lemma 21
.

Let
T:\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
(resp.
\Sh(k_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
) be the
p
-torsion functor.

1.

Both functors
T
are left exact and we have commutative diagrams :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(k_{\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{+}(\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{+}(k_{\tau})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{+}(\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{+}(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RT}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces RT}

2.

For
G\in\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}(k)
(resp.
G\in\Sh(k_{\tau})
), we have
R^{1}T(G)=G/pG
and
R^{q}T(G)=0
for
q\geq 2
.

3.

For
G\in\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
(resp.
G\in\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
) we have :

\displaystyle R\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)}(G,RT(-))

\displaystyle=R\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}(k)}(G,-)

\displaystyle\text{(resp. }R\Hom_{k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p}(G,RT(-))

\displaystyle=R\Hom_{k_{\tau}}(G,-)\text{)}.

4.

The functor
RT:D^{+}(\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}(k))\to D^{+}(\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p))
restricts to a functor between full subcategories
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}(k))\to D^{b}(\Alg_{u}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p))
.

In
(4.)
above, recall that by
[
17
]
(Th. 15.3.1.(i)) the inclusion
\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
, for
\mathcal{A}
an abelian category, identifies
D^{b}(\mathcal{A})
with the full subcategory of
D^{b}(\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A})
of objects with cohomology in
\mathcal{A}
.

Proof of Lemma
21
.

1. Underived case.
Left exactness is obvious. The underived commutative diagram follows from exactness of the Yoneda functors
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
and
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)\to\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
(which itself follows from the exactness of
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
of Proposition
12
and the exactness of filtered colimits in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
).

2. Sheaf case.
For
T:\Sh(k_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
this follows from the description
T=\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(\mathbb{Z}/p,-)
and the projective resolution
0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/p\to 0
.

1. Derived case.
For the diagram of derived functors to commute, since the Yoneda functors are exact, it suffices to show the Yoneda functor sends injectives of
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
to
T
-acyclics in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
. This is due to Proposition
20
and
(2.)
for
T:\Sh(k_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
.

2. Group case.
For
T:\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
this now follows from the diagram of derived categories in
(1.)
and the case of
T:\Sh(k_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p)
.

3.
The functor
T:\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
is right adjoint to inclusion
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)\to\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Hence
T:\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
also has an exact left adjoint, and in particular preserves injectives. The equality now follows by derivation of composite functors and the identifications :

\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,-)=\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,T(-))=\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)}(G,T(-))

for
G\in\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
. The argument in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
is identical.

4.
For
G\in D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
, by
(2.)
,
RT
sends
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
to
D^{b}(\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p))
and by
(1.)
we have a convergent spectral sequence :

E^{ij}_{2}=R^{i}T(H^{j}(G))\Rightarrow R^{i+j}T(G)=H^{i+j}(RT(G)).

By
(2.)
again
R^{i}T(H^{j}(G))\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
for
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
. Since
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
is an abelian full subcategory of
\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
, it follows that
RT(G)
belongs to
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p))
.
∎

Proposition 22
.

The exact, fully faithful Yoneda functor
Y:\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
induces an exact, fully faithful functor of triangulated categories
RY:D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
.

Proof of Proposition
22
.

Because
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D^{b}(\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
is fully faithful and the composite
\varinjlim\mathrm{I}Y:\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\mathrm{I}\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
(still written
Y
) is exact by the proof of Lemma
21
.1, we need to show :

R\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(A,B)=R\Hom_{k_{\mathrm{RP}}}(Y(A),Y(B))

for
A,B\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Furthermore, by Lemma
16
.1 we can assume
A\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)
. Then we have : Lemma
21
.1,3 and Proposition
19
we have :

\displaystyle R\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(A,B)

\displaystyle=R\Hom_{\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k,\mathbb{Z}/p)}(A,RT(B))

(Lemma
21
.3)

\displaystyle=R\Hom_{\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}},\mathbb{Z}/p)}(Y(A),RY(RT(B)))

(Proposition
19
)

\displaystyle=R\Hom_{\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}},\mathbb{Z}/p)}(Y(A),RT(Y(B)))

\displaystyle\text{(Lemma\penalty\ \ref{p-torsion properties}.1 and exactness of }Y\text{)}

\displaystyle=R\Hom_{\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})}(Y(A),Y(B))

(Lemma
21
.3)

which shows
RY:D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
is fully faithful.
∎

Proposition 23
.

Let
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
. Then the following subcategories of
D(k_{\tau})
coincide :

1.

the essential image of
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
by the derived Yoneda functor  ;

2.

the smallest full triangulated subcategory containing
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
(as a complex concentrated in degree
0
)  ;

3.

the full subcategory of bounded objects with cohomology in
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
.

This subcategory is written
D_{0}(k_{\tau})
. The Yoneda functor
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D(k_{\tau})
, and the pushforward of the premorphism of sites
v:k_{\mathrm{RP}}\to k_{\mathrm{RPS}}
given by identity induce exact equivalences of triangulated categories
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}})\cong D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\cong D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
.

Proof of Proposition
23
.

Because
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
is closed under extensions in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
,
(3.)
is a full triangulated subcategory of
D(k_{\tau})
. It contains
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
, so
(3.)
is contained in
(2.)
. To prove conversely that
(2.)
is contained in
(3.)
, it suffices to show every object of
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
(in degree
0
) is contained in
(2.)
, which follows from (the same proof as) Proposition
17
.

Write
D_{0}(k_{\tau})
for the equivalent definitions
(2.)
and
(3.)
. The functor
v_{*}
is exact and defines an equivalence
v_{*}:D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\xrightarrow{\sim}D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
by
[
21
]
(Prop. 2.2 and the discussion preceding it). Clearly the composite
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
is the Yoneda functor, and since all involved functors are exact, the composite
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})\to D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RPS}})
is also the derived Yoneda functor. Hence it only remains to show that
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
defines an equivalence
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\cong D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
.

The Yoneda functor
Y:\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
is exact and fully faithful by Proposition
12
.2. By
[
34
]
(06UR and adjacent discussion) its derived functor
RY:D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
is an exact functor of triangulated categories, and is fully faithful by Proposition
22
. For
G\in D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
,
H^{q}(RY(G))=Y(H^{q}(G))\in\Sh_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
. Hence the essential image of
RY
is contained in
D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
.

Conversely the essential image of
RY
is a full triangulated subcategory of
D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
. Indeed given en exact triangle
Y(A)\to B\to Y(C)\xrightarrow{\delta}Y(A)[1]
in
D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
with
A,C\in D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
, then
B
is determined by the morphism
\delta\in\Hom_{D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})}(Y(C),Y(A)[1])
; the fullness of
RY
ensures
\delta=Y(\delta^{\prime})
for some
\delta^{\prime}:C\to A[1]
, and the exactness of
RY
implies that
B=RY(B^{\prime})
where
B^{\prime}
is the fiber of
\delta^{\prime}
in
D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
. The essential image of
RY
is a full triangulated subcategory containing
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}
, so it contains
D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
. This shows
RY:D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
is an equivalence.
∎

1.5
Duality for RPAU groups

Let
k
be again a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
and let
d=\log_{p}([k:k^{p}])
. Recall that
d
is an integer, the cardinal of any
p
-basis of
k
.

Recall the degree
r\in\mathbb{Z}
, level
n\geq 1
, logarithmic part of the de Rham-Witt complex of
[
19
]
(§4),
\nu_{n}(r)(X)
for
X
an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-scheme whose structure sheaf Zariski-locally a finite
p
-basis (
e.g.

X\in\mathrm{RPSch}/k
, by Lemma
94
.2). Its formation is functorial in
X
and satisfies étale descent, thus it can be viewed as an object
\nu_{n}(r)_{k}
(or simply
\nu_{n}(r)
) of
\Sh(k_{\tau})
for
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
. We write
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}(r)=\nu_{n}(r)[-r]\in D(k_{\tau})
and :

\nu_{\infty}(r)=\varinjlim_{n}\nu_{n}(r),\qquad\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(r)=\varinjlim_{n}\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}(r)=\nu_{\infty}(r)[-r].

This convention is mostly aesthetic, as an effort to unify notations with the Tate twists
\mathbb{Z}/m(r)=\mu_{m}^{\otimes r}
for
m
invertible on the base. We actually define
\mathbb{Z}/m(r)=\mu_{m^{\prime}}^{\otimes r}\oplus\nu_{n}(r)[-r]
when
m=p^{n}m^{\prime}
with
m^{\prime}
prime to
p
. This notation is also partially justified by
[
13
]
.

For our purposes, where only objects of
\mathrm{RPSch}/k
are considered, this convention is sufficient. For arbitrary
X\in\mathrm{Sch}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
, this choice would be inappropriate  ; better candidates for
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}(r)_{X}
are defined in
[
20
]
and
[
28
]
for
X
separated of finite type over a perfect field possibly not regular.

Lemma 24
.

For
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
,
\nu_{n}(r)
defines a relatively perfect wound object of
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
.

Proof of Lemma
24
.

Using Proposition
12
.2 and the exact sequences (
[
19
]
(3.1.5) and (4.1.8)) :

0\to\nu_{n-1}(r)\to\nu_{n}(r)\to\nu_{1}(r)\to 0,\qquad 0\to\nu_{1}(r)\to\Omega^{r}\xrightarrow{C^{-1}-1}\Omega^{r}/B^{r}\Omega\to 0

we inductively reduce to
n=1
then conclude : any
p
-basis of
k
of order
d
gives a
p
-basis of
\mathcal{O}(X)
for any
X\in\mathrm{RPSch}/\mathbb{F}_{p}
, by Lemma
94
.2, which gives a differential basis of that ring, that is
\Omega^{1}\cong(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{d}
and
\Omega^{r}\cong(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})^{\binom{d}{r}}
. That it is wound,
i.e.

\HOM(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}},\nu_{n}(r))=0
, is part of
[
19
]
Th. 3.2.(ii).
∎

Lemma 25
.

Consider
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
,
r\in\mathbb{Z}
and
G\in D^{b}(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
for some
n\geq 1
. Then we have a canonical isomorphism :

R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(G,\nu_{\infty}(r))=R\HOM_{k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}(G,\nu_{n}(r))

where
R\HOM_{k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}
denotes the derived Hom functor for sheaves of
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}
-modules.

Proof of Lemma
25
.

We can assume
G\in\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
. Consider
T_{n}:\Sh(k_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
the
p^{n}
-torsion functor. It is right adjoint to the inclusion functor
J_{n}:\Sh(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
, which is exact, hence
T_{n}
is left exact and preserves injectives. By composition of derived functors it follows that :

R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(J_{n}(G),-)=J_{n}(R\HOM_{k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}(G,RT_{n}(-)).

We have
T_{n}=\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(\mathbb{Z}/p^{n},-)
and the projective resolution
0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{p^{n}}\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}\to 0
in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
identifies
RT_{n}(H)
, for
H\in\Sh(k_{\tau})
, with the complex
H\xrightarrow{p^{n}}H
in degrees
0
and
1
. Hence
R^{q}T_{n}(H)
is identified with
H[p^{n}]
if
q=0
,
H/p^{n}H
if
q=1
, and
0
if
q\geq 2
. With
H=\nu_{\infty}(r)
, we have exact sequences :

0\to\nu_{s}(r)\xrightarrow{j_{s,t}}\nu_{s+t}(r)\xrightarrow{q_{s,t}}\nu_{t}(r)\to 0

where
j_{t,s}\circ q_{s,t}=p^{n}\mathrm{id}_{\nu_{s+t}(r)}
, for
s,t\geq 1
. In particular we have identifications
\nu_{s}(r)=\nu_{s+t}(r)[p^{s}]=p^{t}\nu_{s+t}(r)
. Taking the colimit with respect to
t
(resp.
s
), which is filtered hence exact, we find
\nu_{s}(r)=\nu_{\infty}(r)[p^{s}]
(resp.
\nu_{\infty}(r)=p^{t}\nu_{\infty}(r)
). In particular we have a quasi-isomorphism

\nu_{n}(r)=RT_{n}(\nu_{\infty}(r))
in
D(k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
, thus as desired
R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(J_{n}(G),\nu_{\infty}(r))=J_{n}(R\Hom_{k_{\tau},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}(G,\nu_{n}(r))
.
∎

Theorem 26
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]=p^{d}<+\infty
. Let
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
. For
G\in D_{0}(k_{\tau})
, define
G^{\vee}=R\HOM(G,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d))
.

1.

For all
G\in D_{0}(k_{\tau})
the canonical morphism
G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism.

2.

For
G\in\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
and
q\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
H^{q}(G^{\vee})=0
for
q\neq d,d+1
,
H^{d}(G^{\vee})=H^{d}((G_{w})^{\vee})
is relatively perfect wound, and
H^{d+1}(G^{\vee})=H^{d+1}((G_{s})^{\vee})
is relatively perfect split.

3.

If
G\in D_{0}(k_{\tau})
then for
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we have isomorphisms in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
:

H^{q}(G)_{s}=\EXT^{1}(H^{d+1-q}(G^{\vee})_{s},\nu_{\infty}(d)),\qquad H^{q}(G)_{w}=\HOM(H^{d-q}(G^{\vee})_{w},\nu_{\infty}(d)).

We say that
H^{q}(G)_{s}
and
H^{d+1-q}(G^{\vee})_{s}
(resp.
H^{q}(G)_{w}
and
H^{d-q}(G^{\vee})_{w}
) are perfect Serre duals as split (resp. wound) RPAU
k
-groups.

4.

For
r,n\geq 0
we have
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}(r)^{\vee}\cong\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}(d-r)
. For
G
the relative perfection of a vector group,
G^{\vee}
coincides with the shifted linear dual
\HOM_{k}(G,\Omega^{d})[-d-1]
.

If
(t_{1},\dots,t_{d})
is a
p
-basis of
k
then by Lemma
94
.2 we have
\Omega^{d}\cong\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}\cdot dt_{1}\wedge\dots\wedge dt_{d}
in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
, hence Serre duality for split groups is a generalization of linear duality for vector groups. If
k
is perfect then
\nu_{\infty}(d)=\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, relatively perfect wound groups are the same as finite étale
p
-primary groups, and Serre duality for them coincides with Pontryagin duality.

Proof of Theorem
26
.

The case
\tau=\mathrm{RPS}
is equivalent to the case
\tau=\mathrm{RP}
via Proposition
12
and Proposition
23
. By Lemma
25
and Proposition
16
.1, it suffices to prove the theorem with
D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
replaced by
D(k_{\mathrm{RP}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
,
D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
replaced by the
p^{n}
-torsion subcategory
D_{0}(k_{\mathrm{RP}},\mathbb{Z}/p^{n})
,
R\HOM
replaced by
R\HOM_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}
and correspondingly
(-)^{\vee}
by
R\HOM_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}(-,\nu_{n}(r))
, for all
n\geq 1
.

Then
(1.)
and
(4.)
come from
[
19
]
(Th. 4.3 and Th. 3.2), with our convention
\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}(r)=\nu_{n}(r)[-r]
. Points
(2.)
and
(3.)
come from
[
40
]
(Prop. 3.3 and 3.4).
∎

Remark 27
.

We can instead take
(-)^{\vee}=R\HOM(-,\nu_{\infty}(d))
(or
R\HOM_{\mathbb{Z}/p^{n}}(-,\nu_{n}(d))
for the
p^{n}
-torsion version), as in
[
19
]
and
[
40
]
. Then Theorem
26
.1 holds as-is, while the other points are true after obvious shifts. For instance, Theorem
26
.4 becomes
\nu_{n}(r)^{\vee}\cong\nu_{n}(d-r)
.

1.6
Change of field and trace maps

As before let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]=p^{d}<+\infty
. If
l
is a finite field extension of
k
, we again have
[l:l^{p}]=[k:k^{p}]=p^{d}
(indeed
[l:k^{p}]=[l:l^{p}][l^{p}:k^{p}]=[l:k][l:k^{p}]
and
[l^{p}:k^{p}]=[k:k^{p}]
by injectivity of the Frobenius).

For
R
an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra, by
\Omega^{q}(R)
,
W_{n}\Omega^{q}(R)
,
\nu_{n}(q)(R)
we mean the degree
q
de Rham, de Rham-Witt, logarithmic de Rham-Witt, differentials of
R
, and by
\Omega^{q}_{R}
,
W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{R}
,
\nu_{n}(q)_{R}
the sheaves of degree
q
de Rham, de Rham-Witt, logarithmic de Rham-Witt differentials on
R_{\mathrm{RP}}
or
R_{\tau}
.

Proposition 28
.

Consider
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
and
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
a finite field extension.

1.

Base change by
\pi
defines a morphism of sites
l_{\tau}\to k_{\tau}
, again written
\pi
.

2.

The functor
\pi_{*}:\Sh(l_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
is both left and right adjoint to
\pi^{*}
. In particular,
\pi_{*}
is exact.

3.

If
l/k
is separable then
\pi^{*}:\Sh(l_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
is given by restriction of presheaves to
l_{\tau}\subseteq k_{\tau}
.

4.

If
l/k
