is purely inseparable then
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
define an equivalence
\Sh(k_{\tau})\cong\Sh(l_{\tau})
. Furthermore there exists a purely inseparable finite field extension
\rho:\Spec k\to\Spec l
such that
\pi^{*}=\rho_{*}
.

Proof of Proposition
28
.

The well-definition of
\pi^{-1}:k_{\tau}\to l_{\tau}
is by Lemma
5
.5, and this defines a premorphism of sites by Lemma
5
.2. By
[
34
]
(Lem. 030K), it suffices to prove the other statements in the case
l/k
is separable or purely inseparable.

Separable case.
Because
\pi
is an étale map,
\pi^{\mathrm{set}}
is just given by restriction to the subcategory
l_{\tau}\subseteq k_{\tau}
. In particular it is exact, hence
\pi:l_{\tau}\to k_{\tau}
is a morphism of sites and
\pi^{*}
is described similarly. The proof that
\pi_{*}
is left adjoint to
\pi^{*}
is identical to
[
25
]
(Lem. V.1.12).

Purely inseparable case.
For some
n\geq 0
(say,
n=\log_{p}[l:k]
) we have
l^{p^{n}}\subseteq k
hence the
n
-fold Frobenius
F_{l}^{n}:\Spec l\to\Spec l
factors as a morphism
\rho:\Spec k\to\Spec l
. By Lemma
5
.3-4,
\pi^{-1}:k_{\tau}\to l_{\tau}
is an equivalence with inverse
\rho^{-1}:l_{\tau}\to k_{\tau}
. Thus
\pi_{*}
and
\rho_{*}
define an equivalence
\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(l_{\tau})\cong\Sh^{\mathrm{set}}(k_{\tau})
, so
\pi^{\mathrm{set}}=\rho_{*}
is exact as an equivalence functor, and
\pi
is a morphism of sites. Similarly,
\pi_{*}
and
\rho_{*}
define an equivalence
\Sh(l_{\tau})\cong\Sh(k_{\tau})
, so
\pi^{*}
is an essential inverse to
\pi_{*}
and a fortiori a right adjoint of it.
∎

Proposition 29
.

Consider
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
and
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
a finite field extension. Then
\pi_{*}:\Sh(l_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
and
\pi^{*}:\Sh(k_{\tau})\to\Sh(l_{\tau})
restrict to functors :

\pi_{*}:\Sh_{0}(l_{\tau})\to\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau}),\qquad\pi^{*}:\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})\to\Sh_{0}(l_{\tau}).

If
l/k
is purely inseparable, these define an equivalence
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})\cong\Sh_{0}(l_{\tau})
.

Proof of Corollary
29
.

By
[
34
]
(Lem. 030K), we can assume
l/k
is either separable or purely inseparable. By Proposition
17
and exactness of
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
, it suffices to show
\pi_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{a,l}^{\mathrm{RP}})
belongs to
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
and
\pi^{*}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})
belongs to
\Sh_{0}(l_{\tau})
. For any
X\in k_{\tau}
, we have :

\pi_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{a,l}^{\mathrm{RP}})(X)=\mathbb{G}_{a,l}^{\mathrm{RP}}(X\times_{k}l)=\mathbb{G}_{a,l}(X\times_{k},l)=(\mathrm{Res}_{l/k}\mathbb{G}_{a,l})(X)=(\mathrm{Res}_{l/k}\mathbb{G}_{a,l})^{\mathrm{RP}}(X)

where the first and fourth equalities are by definition of
\pi_{*}
; the second is by Lemma
5
.1, where
X\times_{k}l\in l_{\tau}
by Lemma
5
.5  ; and the third is by definition of the Weil restriction. The group
\mathrm{Res}_{l/k}\mathbb{G}_{a,l}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{[l:k]}
by Example
3
, thus
\pi_{*}(\mathbb{G}_{a,l}^{\mathrm{RP}})=(\mathrm{Res}_{l/k}\mathbb{G}_{a,l})^{\mathrm{RP}}\in\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
as desired.

If
l/k
is separable then
\pi^{*}(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}})=\mathbb{G}_{a,k}^{\mathrm{RP}}\times_{k}\Spec l=(\mathbb{G}_{a,k}\times_{k}\Spec l)^{\mathrm{RP}}=\mathbb{G}_{a,l}^{\mathrm{RP}}
by Lemma
11
and Lemma
5
.2, thus
\pi^{*}
maps
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
to
\Sh_{0}(l_{\tau})
. If
l/k
is purely inseparable, by Proposition
28
.4 we have
\pi^{*}=\rho_{*}
for some purely inseparable finite field extension
\rho:\Spec k\to\Spec l
and
\rho_{*}
sends
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})
to
\Sh_{0}(l_{\tau})
by the previous paragraph. Since
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
define an equivalence
\Sh(k_{\tau})\cong\Sh(l_{\tau})
, their restrictions to subcategories are again an equivalence
\Sh_{0}(k_{\tau})\cong\Sh(l_{\tau})
.
∎

The rest of the section is devoted to Theorem
34
, which compares Serre duality over
k
and over
l
. It is straightforward when
l/k
is separable, but requires some preliminaries for the purely inseparable case.

Recall from
[
23
]
(§2) that an
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R
is
Cartier smooth
if the module of Kähler differentials
\Omega^{1}_{R}
is
R
-flat, the cotangent complex
L_{R/\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is concentrated in degree
0
, and the inverse Cartier operator
C^{-1}:\Omega^{\bullet}_{R}\to H^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\bullet}_{R})
is an isomorphism  ; recall that the latter is the unique graded algebra homomorphism satisfying
C^{-1}(a)=a^{p}
and
C^{-1}(da)=a^{p-1}da
for
a\in R
.

Lemma 30
.

We have the following.

1.

If
R_{0}
is a reduced Cartier smooth
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra whose absolute Frobenius is flat, and
R_{0}\to R
is a flat relatively perfect morphism of
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebras, then
R
is reduced and Cartier smooth.

2.

If
R
is a reduced
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra which admits a finite
p
-basis, then
R
is Cartier smooth.

Proof of Lemma
30
.

1.
Note that
R
is reduced by Lemma
94
.1. By
[
11
, Proposition 3.11]
, the cotangent complex
L_{R/R_{0}}
is acyclic so
L_{R/\mathbb{F}_{p}}\cong R\otimes^{L}_{R_{0}}L_{R_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}
. Since
R
is
R_{0}
-flat and
L_{R_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is concentrated in degree
0
,
L_{R/\mathbb{F}_{p}}
is concentrated in degree
0
. By
R_{0}
-flatness we also have :

\Omega^{1}(R)=H^{0}(L_{R/\mathbb{F}_{p}})=R\otimes_{R_{0}}H^{0}(L_{R_{0}/\mathbb{F}_{p}})=R\otimes_{R_{0}}\Omega^{1}(R_{0})

and
\Omega^{1}(R_{0})
is
R_{0}
-flat so
\Omega^{1}(R)
is
R
-flat. It follows that
\Omega^{q}(R_{0})
is
R_{0}
-flat and
\Omega^{q}(R)=R\otimes_{R_{0}}\Omega^{q}(R_{0})
for all
q\geq 0
. Using the cocartesian square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{R_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{R}}

we have
R^{p}=R\otimes_{R_{0}}R_{0}^{p}
thus
\Omega^{q}(R)=R^{p}\otimes_{R_{0}^{p}}\Omega^{q}(R_{0})
. This is an isomorphism of complexes of
R^{p}
-modules as
q
varies, because exterior differentials are
p
-linear. Because
F_{R_{0}}
is flat,
\Omega^{q}(R_{0})
is
R_{0}^{p}
-flat and we have
H^{q}(\Omega^{\bullet}(R))=R^{p}\otimes_{R_{0}^{p}}H^{q}(\Omega^{\bullet}(R_{0}))
. We have a commutative square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\otimes_{R_{0}}\Omega^{\bullet}(R_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{\bullet}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R^{p}\otimes_{R_{0}^{p}}H^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\bullet}(R_{0}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\bullet}(\Omega^{\bullet}(R))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{R}\otimes C^{-1}_{R_{0}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-1}_{R}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}

where the Frobenius maps
F_{R}
and
F_{R_{0}}
give isomorphisms
R\cong R^{p}
and
R_{0}\cong R_{0}^{p}
because
R
and
R_{0}
are reduced, and
C^{-1}_{R_{0}}
is an isomorphism. Thus
C^{-1}_{R}
is an isomorphism.

2.
Fix a
p
-basis
(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})
of
R
and consider the morphism
R_{0}=\mathbb{F}_{p}[t_{1},\dots,t_{d}]\to R
given by
t_{i}\mapsto x_{i}
. This morphism is relatively perfect by Lemma
94
.2, and flat by
[
19
]
(Prop. 5.2) because the polynomial ring
R_{0}
is Noetherian. The
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra
R_{0}
is clearly reduced and Cartier smooth, thus so is
R
by
(1.)
.
∎

Recall that for
R\to S
a finite syntomic ring homomorphism,
[
34
]
(Lem. 0FLB) defines morphisms
\Theta_{S/R}^{q}:\Omega^{q}(S)\to\Omega^{q}(R)
compatible with base change, such that
\Theta^{0}_{S/R}:S\to R
is the trace map and
\Theta_{S/R}^{\bullet}
is a morphism of differential graded
\Omega^{\bullet}(R)
-modules. For
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
and
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
a finite field extension, as
\pi
is finite syntomic we in particular have a morphism
\Theta_{l/k}^{\bullet}:\pi_{*}\Omega^{\bullet}_{l}\to\Omega^{\bullet}_{k}
of differential graded
\Omega^{\bullet}_{k}
-modules in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
.

Proposition 31
.

Consider
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
,
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
a finite field extension, and
q\geq 0
. There exists a unique family of morphisms in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
:

\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n}=\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n,l/k}:\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}\to\nu_{n}(q)_{k}

for
n\geq 1
, which satisfies
(1.-2.)
below. Additionally it satisfies
(3.-4.)
.

1.

For
n=1
we have a commutative square with the inclusions
\nu_{1}(q)\subseteq\Omega^{q}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{1}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1}(q)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\Omega^{q}_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{q}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{l/k}^{q}}

2.

For
n\geq 1
we have commutative squares with the surjections
\nu_{n+1}(q)\to\nu_{n}(q)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n+1}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n+1}(q)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}(q)_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{n+1}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{n}^{q}}

3.

For
n\geq 1
we have commutative squares with the injections
"p":\nu_{n}(q)\to\nu_{n+1}(q)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}(q)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n+1}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n+1}(q)_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{n}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces"p"}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces"p"}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{n+1}^{q}}

4.

If
\pi^{\prime}:\Spec m\to\Spec l
is a further finite field extension, then
\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n,m/k}=\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n,l/k}\circ\pi_{*}(\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n,m/l})
.

Proof of Proposition
31
.

Uniqueness and (3.-4.)
Consider
\{f_{n}^{q}=\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}\to\nu_{n}(q)_{k}\}_{n\geq 1}
a family of morphisms in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
which satisfies
(2.)
. Then it satisfies
(3.)
: indeed the injections
"p"
are defined by the commutative squares :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n+1}(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n+1}(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}(q)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n+1}(q)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces"p"}

where
s_{n}
denotes the canonical surjection, so we have :

f_{n+1}^{q}\circ\pi_{*}("p")\circ\pi_{*}(s_{n})=f_{n+1}\circ p=p\circ f_{n+1}="p"\circ s_{n}\circ f_{n+1}^{q}="p"\circ f_{n}^{q}\circ\pi_{*}(s_{n}).

By Proposition
28
.2,
\pi_{*}(s_{n})
is a surjection in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
, hence
f_{n+1}^{q}\circ\pi_{*}("p")="p"\circ f_{n}^{q}
, giving
(3.)
. It follows we have commutative diagrams with exact rows given by
[
19
]
(Lem. 4.1.5) :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n+1}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{1}(q)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}(q)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n+1}(q)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1}(q)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}s_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{n+1}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{n}}

so
f_{n+1}^{q}
is determined by
f_{n}^{q}
and
f_{1}^{q}
, and inductively the whole family
\{f_{n}^{q}\}_{n\geq 1}
is determined by
f_{1}^{q}
. This gives uniqueness of a family satisfying
(1.)
and
(2.)
.

Assume families
\{\mathrm{Tr}_{n,l/k}^{q}\}
and
\{\mathrm{Tr}_{n,m/l}^{q}\}
which satisfy
(1.-2.)
exist for both finite extensions
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
and
\pi^{\prime}:\Spec m\to\Spec l
, one easily checks that the family given by
f_{n}^{q}=\mathrm{Tr}_{n,l/k}^{q}\circ(\mathrm{Tr}_{n,m/l}^{q})
satisfies
(2.)
, and it must satisfy
(1.)
because
\Theta^{q}_{m/k}=\Theta^{q}_{l/k}\circ\pi_{*}(\Theta^{q}_{m/l})
. By uniqueness, any family
\{\mathrm{Tr}_{n,m/k}^{q}\}
which satisfies
(1.-2.)
for the finite extension
\Spec m\to\Spec k
, must coincide with
\{f_{n}^{q}\}
, which gives
(4.)
.

Existence.
Consider
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,k}\in\Sh(k_{\tau})
(and similarly
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,l}\in\Sh(l_{\tau})
) the étale sheafification of the presheaf given by the degree
q
algebraic
K
-theory of rings,
X\mapsto K_{q}(\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}))
. Following
[
23
]
(§2.3), when
A
is any (not necessarily local) Cartier-smooth
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebra we can construct a morphism
c_{n}^{q}(A):K_{q}(A)\to\nu_{n}(q)(A)
, which by definition and the discussion of
[
23
]
(§2.3), satisfies the following :

—

c_{n}^{q}
is natural with respect to morphisms of Cartier smooth algebras : in particular, it induces morphisms of sheaves
c^{q}_{n,k}:\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,k}\to\nu_{n}(q)_{k}
and
c^{q}_{n,l}:\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,l}\to\nu_{n}(q)_{l}
, since
\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X})
is Cartier smooth for
X
any relatively perfect
k
- or
l
-scheme, by Lemma
30
;

—

the composite
K_{q}^{M}(A)\to K_{q}(A)\to\nu_{n}(q)(A)
is given as
\{a_{1},\dots,a_{q}\}\mapsto d\log([a_{1}])\wedge\dots\wedge d\log([a_{q}])
.

—

the composite of
c_{n+1}^{q}(A)
with
\nu_{n+1}(q)(A)\to\nu_{n}(q)(A)
is
c_{n}^{q}(A)
;

By
[
23
]
(Th. 2.1),
c_{n}^{q}(A)
induces an isomorphism
K_{q}(A)/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\sim}\nu_{n}(q)(A)
when
A
is Cartier smooth and local. The formations of
K_{q}
and
\nu_{n}(q)
commute with filtered colimits of
k
-algebras, hence the stalk of
c_{n}^{q}
at a geometric point
\overline{x}\to X
of some
X\in k_{\mathrm{RP}}
is
c_{n}^{q}(\mathcal{O}_{X,\overline{x}}^{sh})
. The ring
\mathcal{O}_{X,\overline{x}}^{sh}
is local, and it is relatively perfect over
k
as a filtered colimit of relatively perfect
k
-algebras, hence Cartier smooth. Thus
c_{n}^{q}
induces an isomorphism of sheaves
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,k}/p^{n}\cong\nu_{n}(q)_{k}
and similarly
\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,l}/p^{n}\cong\nu_{n}(q)_{l}
.

On the other hand, for
R
a relatively perfect
k
-algebra with base change
S=R\otimes_{k}l
, there exists a transfer map
\mathrm{Tr}_{K}^{q}(R):K_{q}(S)\to K_{q}(R)
which fits in a commutative square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K_{q}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{q}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{q}(R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{K}^{q}(R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\log}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\log}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{q}_{S/R}}

where the vertical maps are the Chern characters, extending the differential logarithms
K^{M}_{q}(-)\to K_{q}(-)\to\Omega^{q}_{\log}
; both maps
\mathrm{Tr}_{K}^{q}
and
d\log
and this compatibility are established in
[
31
]
(p. 393-394) for any finite syntomic morphism of Noetherian rings
R\to S
, and extended to our case by writing
R=\varinjlim_{\lambda}R_{\lambda}
and
S=\varinjlim_{\lambda}R_{\lambda}\otimes_{k}l
, with
R_{\lambda}
a filtered system of Noetherian
k
-algebras. Note that
d\log:K_{q}(A)/p\to\Omega^{q}(A)
coincides with
c^{q}_{1}(A):K_{q}(A)/p\xrightarrow{\sim}\nu_{1}(q)(A)
for local Cartier smooth
A
by the description of
d\log
on symbols (and the fact that,
c^{q}_{1}(A)
being an isomorphism,
K_{q}(A)/p
is generated by symbols).

The Chern characters and
\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{K}(R)
are compatible with base change, thus after sheafification induce a commutative square in
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\mathcal{K}_{q,l}^{\mathrm{et}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\Omega^{q}_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{q}_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{K}^{q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\log}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d\log}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{q}_{l/k}}

We define
\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n}:\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}\to\nu_{n}(q)_{k}
as the composite :

\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}(c^{q}_{n})^{-1}}\pi_{*}(\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,l}/p^{n})=\pi_{*}(\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,l})/p^{n}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{K}}\mathcal{K}^{\mathrm{et}}_{q,k}/p^{n}\xrightarrow{c_{n}^{q}}\nu_{n}(q)_{k}

where we use the exactness of
\pi_{*}
. Property
(1.)
follows from the compatibility of
\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{K}
with
\Theta^{q}_{l/k}
and the identification of
c_{1}^{q}
with
d\log
. Property
(2.)
follows from the compatibility of
c_{n}^{q}
with the surjections
\nu_{n+1}(q)\to\nu_{n}(q)
, and the fact that
\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{K}
does not depend on
n
.
∎

Remark 32
.

The above Proposition
31
and its proof hold
verbatim
if we replace
k
and
l
by any reduced Cartier smooth
\mathbb{F}_{p}
-algebras with flat absolute Frobenius maps, restrict
k_{\tau}
and
l_{\tau}
to only flat schemes, and
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
to be any finite syntomic map such that
\pi_{*}:\Sh(l_{\tau})\to\Sh(k_{\tau})
is exact. The restriction to flat schemes is superfluous if
k
is Noetherian, by
[
19
]
(Prop. 5.2), but the exactness of
\pi_{*}
is used both for uniqueness and existence. This is the case if
\pi
is a composite finite étale maps and finite universal homeomorphisms (the proof is identical to Proposition
28
: the finite étale case is classical, and in the finite universal homeomorphism case by
[
22
]
(Cor. 20) and Lemma
5
.3-4,
\pi_{*}
is an equivalence).

Lemma 33
.

Consider
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
and
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
a purely inseparable finite field extension. Then the map
\mathrm{Tr}=\varinjlim_{n}\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{n}:\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}\to\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}
of Proposition
31
(where
d=\log_{p}[k:k^{p}]
) is an isomorphism in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
.

Note that the same lemma generally does not hold for
l/k
separable or for
\mathrm{Tr}^{q}_{n}
with
q\neq d
.

Proof of Lemma
33
.

By Proposition
31
.4 and
[
34
]
(Lem. 09HI), we can assume
l=k[\beta]
with
\beta^{p}=\alpha\in k\setminus k^{p}
. By Proposition
31
.2-3 and
[
19
]
(Lem. 4.1.5), we have commutative diagrams with exact rows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n-1}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{1}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n-1}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{n}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{n-1}|_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{n}|_{\nu}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{1}|_{\nu_{1}(d)}}

which reduces to showing
\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{1}
is an isomorphism. This can be checked at stalks,
i.e.
for
\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{1}(R):\nu_{1}(d)(S)\to\nu_{1}(d)(R)
where
R
is a strictly Henselian relatively perfect
k
-algebra and
S=R\otimes_{k}l=R[\beta]
. Then by Proposition
31
.2, the map
\mathrm{Tr}^{d}_{1}(R)
coincides with a morphism
\Theta=\Theta^{d}_{S/R}:\Omega^{d}(S)\to\Omega^{d}(R)
such that :

\displaystyle\Theta(u\beta^{i}d\log(\beta)\wedge dx_{2}\wedge\dots\wedge dx_{d})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\text{if }i\neq 0

\displaystyle=ud\log(\alpha)\wedge dx_{2}\wedge\dots\wedge dx_{d}

\displaystyle\text{if }i=0

for all
0\leq i<p
and
u,x_{2},\dots,x_{d}\in R
(see
[
34
]
(Rem. 0FLF) with the facts that
\beta\in S^{\times}
,
\alpha\in R^{\times}
).

Write
B^{d}=\im(d:\Omega^{d-1}\to\Omega^{d})
the cocycles,
q:\Omega^{d}\to\Omega^{d}/B^{d}
the quotient, and
C^{-1}:\Omega^{d}\to\Omega^{d}/B^{d}
the inverse Cartier operator. Because
R
and
S
are (finite products of) strictly Henselian local rings, we have exact sequences :

0\to\nu_{1}(d)(A)\to\Omega^{q}(A)\xrightarrow{C^{-1}_{A}-q_{A}}\Omega^{d}(A)/B^{d}(A)\to 0

for
A=R,S
, by
[
19
]
(Lem. 4.1.5). We define
\tilde{\Theta}:\Omega^{d}(S)/B^{d}(S)\to\Omega^{d}(R)/B^{d}(R)
as the morphism induced by
\Theta
under these quotients. Consider the following claims :

(i)

The morphism
\Theta:\Omega^{d}(S)\to\Omega^{d}(R)
is surjective.

(ii)

We have
q_{R}\circ\Theta=\tilde{\Theta}\circ q_{S}
and
C^{-1}_{R}\circ\Theta=\tilde{\Theta}\circ C^{-1}_{S}
.

(iii)

We have
\ker(\Theta)\subseteq B^{d}(S)\subseteq\Omega^{d}(S)
.

Assuming these, the bijectivity of
\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{d}(R):\nu_{1}(d)(S)\to\nu_{1}(d)(R)
follows. Indeed we have a commutative diagram with exact rows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1}(d)(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d}(S)/B^{d}(S)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{1}(d)(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Omega^{d}(R)/B^{d}(R)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{d}(R)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-1}_{S}-q_{S}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\Theta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{-1}_{R}-q_{R}}

which by (i) and the snake lemma, induces an exact sequence :

0\to\ker(\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{d}(R))\to\ker(\Theta)\xrightarrow{C^{-1}_{S}-q_{S}}\ker(\tilde{\Theta})\to\coker(\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{d}(R))\to 0.

By (iii) the map
\ker(\Theta)\xrightarrow{C^{-1}_{S}-q_{S}}\ker(\tilde{\Theta})
is simply the inverse Cartier operator
\ker(\Theta)\xrightarrow{C^{-1}_{S}}\ker(\tilde{\Theta})
, which is an isomorphism by (ii) and Lemma
30
. Hence
\ker(\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{d}(R))=\coker(\mathrm{Tr}_{1}^{d}(R))=0
.

We now prove (i-iii). We start with preliminary computations of the modules of differentials. By
[
34
]
(Lem. 07P2), the
p
-independant family
\{\alpha\}
of
k
can be extended to a
p
-basis
(\alpha,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
of
k
. One easily checks that
(\beta,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
is a
p
-basis of
l
. Hence
(\alpha,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
is a
p
-basis of
R
and
(\beta,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
is a
p
-basis of
S
, by Lemma
94
.2. We shall write :

x^{m}=x_{2}^{m_{2}}\cdots x_{d}^{m_{d}},\qquad dx=dx_{2}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{d},\qquad d\log(x)=x_{2}^{-1}\cdots x_{d}^{-1}dx

for
m=(m_{2},\dots,m_{d})\in I
where
I=\{(m_{2},\dots,m_{d});0\leq m_{i}<p\}
.

Modules
\Omega^{d}
and
\Omega^{d}/B^{d}
and map
q
.
Fix
A=R,S
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})=(\alpha,x_{2},\dots,x_{d}),(\beta,x_{2},\dots,x_{d})
correspondingly. Then
y_{i}\in A
is a unit, since it comes from either field
k
or
l
. We write :

y^{m}=y_{1}^{m_{1}}\dots y_{d}^{m_{d}},\qquad d\log(y_{J})=d\log(y_{j_{1}})\wedge\cdots\wedge d\log(y_{j_{r}}),\qquad d\log(y)=d\log(y_{1})\wedge\cdots\wedge d\log(y_{d})

for
m=(m_{1},\dots,m_{d})\in I^{\prime}=\{(m_{1},\dots,m_{d});0\leq m_{i}<p\}
and
J=\{1\leq j_{1}<\dots<j_{r}\leq d\}
a set of distinct indices. By
[
24
]
(38.A),
(dy_{1},dy_{2},\dots,dy_{d})
is a free basis of the
A
-module
\Omega^{1}(A)
; this is unchanged by multiplying each term by a unit, so
(d\log(y_{1}),\dots,d\log(y_{d}))
is again a free
A
-basis of
\Omega^{1}(A)
. It follows that we have identifications for
0\leq r\leq d
:

\displaystyle\Omega^{r}(A)

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{|J|=r}A\cdot d\log(y_{J})

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }A,

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{|J|=r}\bigoplus_{m\in I^{\prime}}A^{p}\cdot y^{m}d\log(y_{J})

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }A^{p}.

In particular we have identifications :

\displaystyle\Omega^{d}(S)

\displaystyle=S\cdot d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }S,

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{0\leq n<p}R\cdot\beta^{n}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }R,

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{0\leq n<p}\bigoplus_{m\in I}S^{p}\cdot\beta^{n}x^{m}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }S^{p}=R^{p}[\alpha],

and similarly over
R
:

\displaystyle\Omega^{d}(R)

\displaystyle=R\cdot d\log(\alpha)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }R,

\displaystyle=\bigoplus_{0\leq n<p}\bigoplus_{m\in I}R^{p}\cdot\alpha^{n}x^{m}d\log(\alpha)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\text{as a module over }R^{p}.

Consider an
A^{p}
-linear basis element
\omega=y^{m}d\log(y_{\hat{i}})
in
\Omega^{d-1}(A)
, where
\hat{i}=\{1\leq j\leq d;j\neq i\}
. Then :

d\omega=(-1)^{i-1}m_{i}y^{m}d\log(y)

where
(-1)^{i-1}m_{i}
is either
0
or a unit in
A
. Hence
B^{d}(A)
is exactly the
A^{p}
-linear submodule of
\Omega^{d}(A)
spanned by those basis elements
y^{m}d\log(y)
with
m\neq(0,\dots,0)
. It follows that we have identifications :

\Omega^{d}(S)/B^{d}(S)=S^{p}d\log(\beta)d\log(x),\qquad\Omega^{d}(R)/B^{d}(R)=R^{p}d\log(\alpha)d\log(x)

such that the projection
q:\Omega^{d}\to\Omega^{d}/B^{d}
is the projection onto the
(n,m)=(0,(0,\dots,0))
-th coordinate.

Inverse Cartier operators.
Take as before
A=R,S
and
(y_{1},\dots,y_{d})
the fixed
p
-basis of
A
. The inverse Cartier operator
C^{-1}(A):\Omega^{\bullet}(A)\to\Omega^{\bullet}(A)/B^{\bullet}(A)
is characterized as a ring morphism satisfying :

C^{-1}(a)=a^{p},\qquad C^{-1}(da)=a^{p-1}da

for any
a\in A
; if
a\in A^{\times}
we thus have
C^{-1}(d\log(a))=d\log(a)
. Hence in degree
d
it is the map :

C^{-1}(A):\Omega^{d}(A)=A\cdot d\log(y)\to\Omega^{d}(A)/B^{d}(A)=A^{p}\cdot d\log(y),\qquad ad\log(y)\mapsto a^{p}d\log(y).

Trace morphisms.
By
[
34
]
(Lem. 0AX5), the map
\Theta:\Omega^{d}(S)\to\Omega^{d}(R)
is given by projecting onto the
R
-linear coordinate
n=0
:

\displaystyle\Theta:\Omega^{d}(S)=\bigoplus_{0\leq n<p}R\cdot\beta^{n}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{d}(R)=R\cdot d\log(\alpha)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}a_{n}\beta^{n}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\mapsto a_{0}d\log(\alpha)d\log(x).

From this description,
\Theta
is clearly surjective, proving (i). The kernel of
\Theta
is spanned by basis elements
\beta^{n}x^{m}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)
with
n\neq 0
, thus contained in the kernel of
q_{S}
(spanned by those
\beta^{n}x^{m}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)
with
n\neq 0
or
m\neq(0,\dots,0)
), proving (iii). Since
C^{-1}_{S}-q_{S}
is surjective, the map
\tilde{\Theta}
is characterized by the relation
\tilde{\Theta}\circ(C^{-1}_{S}-q_{S})=(C^{-1}_{R}-q_{R})\circ\Theta
. One checks that, under the identification of free
R^{p}
-modules :

\Omega^{d}(S)/B^{d}(S)=S^{p}\cdot d\log(\beta)d\log(x)=R^{p}[\alpha]\cdot d\log(\beta)d\log(x)=\bigoplus_{0\leq n<p}R^{p}\cdot\alpha^{n}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

one can choose
\tilde{\Theta}
to be the projection onto the coordinate
n=0
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}:\Omega^{d}(S)/B^{d}(S)=\bigoplus_{0\leq n<p}R^{p}\cdot\alpha^{n}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\to\Omega^{d}(R)/B^{d}(R)=R^{p}\cdot d\log(\alpha)d\log(x),

\displaystyle\sum_{n=0}^{p-1}a_{n}^{p}\alpha^{n}d\log(\beta)d\log(x)

\displaystyle\mapsto a_{0}^{p}d\log(\alpha)d\log(x).

from which (ii) follows easily. This concludes.
∎

Theorem 34
.

Consider
\tau=\mathrm{RP},\mathrm{RPS}
and
\pi:\Spec l\to\Spec k
a finite field extension. There exist maps :

\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\xrightarrow{\eta}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l},\quad\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k},

in
\Sh(l_{\tau})
and
\Sh(k_{\tau})
, and correspondingly
\pi^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l})
in
D(l_{\tau})
and
\pi_{\infty}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}
in
D(k_{\tau})
, with the following properties.

1.

The map
\eta:\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\to\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}
is an isomorphism, and the
composite
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}\eta}\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}
coincides with the counit morphism
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\to\mathrm{id}
.

2.

If
G\otimes^{L}H\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}
is a perfect pairing in
D(k_{\tau})
then we have a perfect pairing in
D(k_{\tau})
:

(\pi^{*}G)\otimes^{L}(\pi^{*}H)=\pi^{*}(G\otimes^{L}H)\to\pi^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}\xrightarrow{\eta}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l}.

3.

If
G\otimes^{L}H\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l}
is a perfect pairing in
D(l_{\tau})
then we have a perfect pairing in
D(k_{\tau})
:

(\pi_{*}G)\otimes^{L}(\pi_{*}H)\to\pi_{*}(G\otimes^{L}H)\to\pi_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Tr}}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}.

4.

Given
G\otimes^{L}H\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}
a pairing in
D(k_{\tau})
, we have a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{*}\pi^{*}G)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\pi_{*}\pi^{*}H)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}}

where the vertical maps
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\to\mathrm{id}
and
\mathrm{id}\to\pi_{*}\pi^{*}
are the counit and unit.

Actually we do not claim that the map
\mathrm{Tr}
in the theorem above is always the same as that of Proposition
31
; the latter is used only to treat the purely inseparable part of the extension
l/k
, while the separable part can be treated with more straightforward methods.

Proof of Theorem
34
.

By
[
34
]
(Lem. 030K), we can assume
l/k
is either separable or purely inseparable.

Separable case.
Recall that
\nu_{n}(q)_{k}
for
q\in\mathbb{Z}
is the subsheaf of the sheaf of de Rham-Witt differentials :

W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k}:X\in k_{\tau}\mapsto W_{n}\Omega^{q}(\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{X}))

whose sections are elements which are étale-locally sums of elements form
d\log(x_{1})\wedge\dots\wedge d\log(x_{q})
with
x_{i}\in\Gamma(X,\mathbb{G}_{m})
. By Proposition
28
.3,
\pi^{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k}
is the restriction of
W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k}
to the subcategory
l_{\tau}\subseteq k_{\tau}
. Evidently, this coincides with
W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{l}
and correspondingly the subsheaves
\pi_{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{k}\subseteq\pi^{*}W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{k}
and
\nu_{n}(q)_{l}\subseteq W_{n}\Omega^{q}_{l}
coincide also. This defines an isomorphism
\eta^{q}_{n}:\pi^{*}\nu_{n}(q)_{k}\xrightarrow{\sim}\nu_{n}(q)_{l}
for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
n\geq 1
, which further induces an isomorphism
\eta=\varinjlim_{n}\eta^{d}_{n}:\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\to\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}
.

Define
\mathrm{Tr}
as the composite
\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}\xrightarrow{\pi_{*}\eta^{-1}}\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\to\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}
. Then
(1.)
is clear.

For
(2.)
, we have :

\displaystyle\Hom_{D(l_{\tau})}(T,\pi^{*}R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(A,B))

\displaystyle=\Hom_{D(k_{\tau})}((\pi_{*}T)\otimes^{L}A,B)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{D(k_{\tau})}(\pi_{*}(T\otimes^{L}\pi^{*}A),B)

\displaystyle=\Hom_{D(l_{\tau})}(T,R\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(\pi^{*}A,\pi^{*}B))

for
A,B\in D(k_{\tau})
and
T\in D(l_{\tau})
, where the first and third equalities are by adjunctions (using Proposition
28
.2), and the second is the projection formula (which can be proven in this context by the same argument as
[
34
]
Lem. 0DK5). It follows that

\pi^{*}R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(A,B)=R\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(\pi^{*}A,\pi^{*}B)
, and
(2.)
follows by taking
A=G
,
B=\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}
, and composing with the isomorphism
\eta:\pi^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{l}
.

For
(3.)
, the composite :

\pi_{*}R\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(A,\pi^{*}B)\xrightarrow{\sim}R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(\pi_{*}A,\pi_{*}\pi^{*}B)\to R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(\pi_{*}A,B)

is an isomorphism for
A\in D(l_{\tau})
and
B\in D(k_{\tau})
, where the first map is obtained by deriving the composite functor
\pi_{*}\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(A,\pi^{*}(-))=\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(\pi_{*}A,\pi_{*}\pi^{*}(-))
, wherein
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
are exact and preserve injectives (by Proposition
28
.2), and the second is the counit
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\to\mathrm{id}
; the proof is identical to
[
25
]
(Prop. V.1.13).

Point
(4.)
follows from
(1.)
because we have a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes^{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}(d)_{k}}

where the vertical maps are (co)units and the lower pairing is induced by the upper pairing. Indeed it suffices to check the commutativity of :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}A_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\otimes}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}B_{0}(X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}C_{0}(X)}

for
A_{0}\otimes B_{0}\to C_{0}
a pairing in
\Sh(k_{\tau})
and
X\in k_{\tau}
. This can be checked explicitly by picking a Galois extension
l^{\prime}/k
containing
l
and using the explicit description of
\mathrm{Tr}
as in the proof of
[
25
]
Prop. V.1.12.

Purely inseparable case.
The morphism
\mathrm{Tr}:\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}\to\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}
is the isomorphism of Lemma
33
. Recall that
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
form an equivalence
\Sh(k_{\tau})\cong\Sh(l_{\tau})
and define :

\eta:\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\xrightarrow{\pi^{*}\mathrm{Tr}^{-1}}\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}\xrightarrow{\sim}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}.

Clearly
\eta
is an isomorphism and we have a commutative diagram :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}nu_{\infty}(d)_{l}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\mathrm{Tr}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{*}\eta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sim}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Tr}^{-1}}

so that
\mathrm{Tr}\circ\pi_{*}\eta
is the counit
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}\to\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}
followed by
\mathrm{Tr}\circ\mathrm{Tr}^{-1}=\mathrm{id}_{\nu_{\infty}(d)_{k}}
, proving
(1.)
. Because
\pi:l_{\tau}\to k_{\tau}
is a morphism of sites,
\pi^{*}:D(k_{\tau})\to D(l_{\tau})
is monoidal hence its essential inverse
\pi_{*}:D(l_{\tau})\to D(k_{\tau})
is monoidal as well. It follows that we have natural isomorphisms :

\displaystyle\pi^{*}R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(-,-)

\displaystyle=R\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(\pi^{*}(-),\pi^{*}(-))

\displaystyle\pi_{*}R\HOM_{k_{\tau}}(-,\pi^{*}(-))

\displaystyle=R\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(\pi_{*}(-),\pi_{*}\pi^{*}(-))=R\HOM_{l_{\tau}}(\pi_{*}(-),-)

and the proofs of
(2.)
and
(3.)
follow just like in the separable case. Point
(4.)
follows from the monoidality of
\pi_{*}
and
\pi^{*}
and the fact that the counit and unit morphisms
\pi_{*}\pi^{*}\to\mathrm{id}
,
\mathrm{id}\to\pi_{*}\pi^{*}
are inverse maps.
∎

2
Ind-pro-RPAU groups

2.1
Reminders on ind-pro-categories

For
\mathcal{A}
a category, we write
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
its ind-category and
\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}=(\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}^{\mathrm{op}})^{\mathrm{op}}
its pro-category. We refer to
[
17
]
(§6, 8.6, 15) and
[
36
]
(§2.2) for all details pertaining to them  ; we recall the key points here.

Definition.
Let
\mathcal{U}
be a fixed universe containing
\mathbb{N}
. Objects of
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
are filtered
\mathcal{U}
-small diagrams
\{X_{i}\}_{i\in I}
of objects of
\mathcal{A}
, viewed as formal filtered colimits
\varinjlim_{i\in I}X_{i}
. Given such
X=\varinjlim_{i}X_{i}
and
Y=\varinjlim_{j}Y_{j}
in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
, with
X_{i},Y_{j}\in\mathcal{A}
, morphisms
X\to Y
are defined by :

\Hom_{\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}}\left(\varinjlim_{i}X_{i},\varinjlim_{j}Y_{j}\right)=\varprojlim_{i}\varinjlim_{j}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{i},Y_{j}).

The category
\mathcal{A}
fully faithfully embeds in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
. The category
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
has all
\mathcal{U}
-small filtered colimits, and objects of
\mathcal{A}
are exactly the compacts of
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
. If
X=\{X_{i}\}_{i}
is a filtered diagram of
\mathcal{A}
then we genuinely have a colimit
X=\varinjlim_{i}X_{i}
in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
. The formation of
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
is functorial in
\mathcal{A}
, and if
\mathcal{A}
has
\mathcal{U}
-small filtered colimits then their computation in
\mathcal{A}
defines a functor
\varinjlim:\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
left adjoint to the inclusion
\mathcal{A}\to\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
.

Abelian case.
If
\mathcal{A}
is abelian then so is
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
and any short exact sequence in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
arises as a filtered colimit of short exact sequences of
\mathcal{A}
(
[
17
]
Prop. 8.6.5 and 8.6.6). Filtered colimits are exact in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
, and the formation of
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
takes left (resp. right) exact functors to left (resp. right) exact functors of ind-categories. (
[
17
]
Prop. 8.6.8) If
\mathcal{A}
is abelian and small, then
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
is a Grothendieck category hence admits enough injectives (
[
17
]
Th. 8.6.5). In general
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
does not have enough injectives (
[
17
]
Cor. 15.1.3). However if
F:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
is a left (resp. right) exact functor of abelian categories and
\mathcal{A}
has enough
F
-injectives (resp.
F
-projectives) then
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
has enough
\mathrm{I}F
-injectives (resp.
\mathrm{I}F
-projectives) and
R^{q}(\mathrm{I}F)=\mathrm{I}(R^{q}F)
(resp.
L_{q}(\mathrm{I}F)=\mathrm{I}(L_{q}F)
) for
q\in\mathbb{Z}
(
[
17
]
Prop. 15.3.2 and 15.3.7).

Derived Hom-functor.
We will primarily consider categories of the form
\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}
where
\mathcal{A}
is an essentially small abelian category (either the category of RPAU groups over a perfect field, or a category of finite abelian groups). Then
[
36
]
(§2.2, particularly Prop. 2.2.2) shows the derived functor
R\Hom_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}:D^{-}(\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\times D^{+}(\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A})\to D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})
exists and provides a method to compute it. Namely we have spectral sequences and equalities :

\displaystyle E^{pq}_{2}=R^{p}\varprojlim_{i}\Ext^{q}_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}(X_{i},Y)\Rightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}(\varinjlim_{i}X_{i},Y)

\displaystyle\Ext^{n}_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},\varinjlim_{k}Y_{k})=\varinjlim_{k}\Ext^{n}_{\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},Y_{k})

{}^{\prime}E^{pq}_{2}=R^{p}\varprojlim_{l}\Ext^{q}_{\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},Y_{l})\Rightarrow\Ext^{p+q}_{\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},\varprojlim_{l}Y_{l})

\displaystyle\Ext^{n}_{\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}}(\varprojlim_{j}X_{j},Y_{0})=\varinjlim_{j}\Ext^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{j},Y_{0})

whenever
Y\in\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}
,
X_{i},X_{0},Y_{k}\in\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}
, and
X_{j},Y_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
.

This will often be summarized by writing, for
X_{ij},Y_{kl}\in\mathcal{A}
:

R\Hom_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}(\varinjlim_{i}\varprojlim_{j}X_{ij},\varinjlim_{k}\varprojlim_{l}Y_{kl})=R\varprojlim_{i}\varinjlim_{k}R\varprojlim_{l}\varinjlim_{j}R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{ij},Y_{kl}).

Note that the inverse limits inside
R\Hom_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}
are underived. This equality leads to a correct understanding when considering Ext’s, as illustrated by the previous spectral sequences, but may be misleading :

First,
\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}
may not genuinely have a derived functor
R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}:D^{-}(\mathcal{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\times D^{+}(\mathcal{A})\to D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})
. Instead for
X_{0},Y_{0}\in\mathcal{A}
we define
R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},Y_{0})=R\Hom_{\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},Y_{0})\in D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})
. With this we still have
H^{n}R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},Y_{0})=\Ext^{n}_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{0},Y_{0})
(Ext-group in the sense of Yoneda), so this recovers the actual derived
R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}
if it exists, and poses no problem when computing cohomology, though we do not assume
R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}
exists (and in our cases of interest
\mathcal{A}=\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k),\mathrm{Ab}_{\mathrm{fin}}
it will not).

Second,
R\varprojlim
and
\varinjlim
do not denote genuine (derived) (co)limits in the derived category
D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})
. Varying
i,j,k,l
we can obtain diagrams of objects
R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(X_{ij},Y_{kl})\in D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})
, and the right-hand term of the above equality misleadingly suggests taking successive (derived) (co)limits of these diagrams, as a composite :

(\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}\xrightarrow{R\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})\xrightarrow{R\varprojlim(\mathrm{P}\varinjlim)(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}R\varprojlim)(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\varinjlim)}D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab}).

where
\varinjlim
and
R\varprojlim
denote (derived) (co)limit-computing functors. We do not claim that such a computation makes sense or recovers
R\Hom_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}
. What is true is we have a factorization of
R\Hom_{\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}}
as :

\displaystyle D^{-}(\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A})^{\mathrm{op}}\times D^{+}(\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{R\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}}D^{+}(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Ab})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\varinjlim}D^{+}(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Ab})

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{R\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\varprojlim}D^{+}(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Ab})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{P}\varinjlim}D^{+}(\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Ab})\xrightarrow{R\varprojlim}D^{+}(\mathrm{Ab})

where
\varinjlim:\mathrm{I}\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
and
\varprojlim:\mathrm{P}\mathcal{B}\to\mathcal{B}
are the (co)limit-computing functors for
\mathcal{B}=\mathrm{Ab},\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Ab},\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Ab},\dots
Then
\varinjlim,\mathrm{P}\varinjlim,\mathrm{I}\mathrm{P}\varinjlim,\dots
are exact and
\varprojlim,\mathrm{I}\varprojlim,\mathrm{P}\mathrm{I}\varprojlim,\dots
are left-exact, so they admit derived functors as in the above composition. If
F
is a left-exact functor of abelian categories with enough
F
-injectives, we have
R^{n}\mathrm{I}F=\mathrm{I}R^{n}F
and
R^{n}\mathrm{P}F=\mathrm{P}R^{n}F
, so again subtleties vanish when only considering cohomology.

2.2
Ind-pro-RPAU groups and
\mathcal{W}_{k}

Definition 35
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. We say a group
G\in\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is :

—

Type 1
if
G\in\mathrm{PAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\subseteq\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is an
\mathbb{N}
-indexed limit
\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}
where each
G_{n}\in\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}_{u}(k)
is connected and the transition morphisms
G_{n+1}\to G_{n}
are surjective with connected kernels  ;

—

Type 2
if
G\in\mathrm{IAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\subseteq\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is an
\mathbb{N}
-indexed colimit
\varinjlim_{n}G_{n}
where each
G_{n}\in\Alg^{\mathrm{RP}}_{u}(k)
is connected and the transition morphisms
G_{n}\to G_{n+1}
are injective.

Such an inverse (resp. direct)
\mathbb{N}
-indexed system will be called a
type 1 inverse system
(resp.
type 2 direct system
). We define
\mathcal{W}_{k}
as the full subcategory of
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
of objects
G
which admit a filtration
G^{\prime\prime}\leq G^{\prime}\leq G
such that
G^{\prime\prime}
is type 1,
G^{\prime}/G^{\prime\prime}
is type 2, and
G/G^{\prime}
is finite étale
p
-primary. We say such an object of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is
connected
if we can in addition choose
G=G^{\prime}
.

Note that the transitions in a type 1 inverse system are automatically affine by
[
34
]
(Lem. 01SH).

The category
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is not abelian. It is however stable under extensions in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, which is the following result. The proof breaks down into many similar cases, making it long, but it is not very technical.
1
1

1

We thank Takashi Suzuki for explaining this fact in private communication, which was not proven in his papers.

Lemma 36
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a small abelian category and
\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathcal{A}
a full subcategory closed under extensions. Then
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
,
\mathrm{P}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}
,
\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{A}
are full subcategories closed under extensions.

Proof of Lemma
36
.

Let
0\to H\to E\to G\to 0
be an exact sequence in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
where
H=\varinjlim_{j}H_{j}
and
G=\varinjlim_{i}G_{i}
with
H_{j},G_{i}\in\mathcal{B}
. Define
E_{i}
as the pullback of
E
along
G_{i}\to G
. Then we have an exact sequence
0\to H\to E_{i}\to G_{i}\to 0
and
E=\varinjlim_{i}E_{i}
. By
[
36
]
(Prop. 2.2.2, which uses smallness) we have :

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G_{i},H)=\varinjlim_{k}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{PAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G_{i},H_{j})

so for some
j_{0}
we have an exact sequence
0\to H_{j_{0}}\to E_{i,j_{0}}\to G_{i}\to 0
whose pushout along
H_{j_{0}}\to H
is
0\to H\to E_{i}\to G_{i}
. Defining
E_{i,j}
as the pushout of
E_{i,j_{0}}
along
H_{j_{0}}\to H_{j}
for
j\geq j_{0}
, we get
E_{i}=\varinjlim_{j\geq j_{0}}E_{i,j}
. Because
\mathcal{B}
is closed under extensions, we have
E_{i,j}\in\mathcal{B}
hence
E=\varinjlim_{i,j\geq j_{0}}E_{i,j}\in\mathrm{I}\mathcal{B}
.

Thus
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
is closed under extensions. Dually,
\mathrm{P}\mathcal{B}\subseteq\mathrm{P}\mathcal{A}
is also closed under extensions, and the ind-pro-result follows by successively applying the ind- and pro-results.
∎

Lemma 37
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. Then the full subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{k}\subseteq\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is stable under extensions. An extension in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
of type 1, type 2, or connected objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
is again type 1, type 2 or connected in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
, respectively.

Proof of Proposition
37
.

Let
0\to H\to E\to G\to 0
be an exact sequence in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
with
G,H\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
. We need to show
E\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
. We work case by case.

1. Case
H
and
G
are connected.
Consider
\mathcal{C}\subseteq\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
the full subcategory of connected objects. It is closed under extensions and as such is an exact category, where all monics are admissible and admissible epics are surjections with connected kernel. Type 1 (resp. type 2) objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
are the same as admissible objects of
\mathrm{P}\mathcal{C}
(resp.
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{C}
) of at most countable size, in the sense of
[
4
]
(Def. 3.2, Def. 4.1, Prop. 3.18), and the full subcategory
\mathcal{W}_{k}^{0}\subseteq\mathcal{W}_{k}
of connected objects is exactly the category of elementary Tate objects of
\mathcal{C}
of at most countable size in the sense of
[
4
]
(Th. 5.4, the proof of which shows any elementary Tate object admits an admissible pro-subobject with admissible ind-quotient). By
[
4
]
(Th. 5.4),
\mathcal{W}_{k}^{0}
is closed under extensions in
\mathrm{Ind}^{a}_{\aleph_{0}}(\mathrm{Pro}^{a}_{\aleph_{0}}(\mathcal{C}))
.

By
[
4
]
(Th. 3.7), for
\mathcal{A}
an exact category,
\mathrm{Ind}^{a}_{\aleph_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
is closed under extensions in the category of left-exact presheaves on
\mathcal{A}
, which contains
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
as an exact subcategory. It follows that
\mathrm{Ind}^{a}_{\aleph_{0}}(\mathcal{A})
is closed under extensions in
\mathrm{I}\mathcal{A}
. Thus
\mathcal{W}_{k}^{0}
is closed under extensions in
\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{C}
. Finally by Lemma
36
,
\mathrm{IP}\mathcal{C}
is closed under extensions in
\mathrm{IP}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Thus
\mathcal{W}_{k}^{0}
is closed under extensions in
\mathrm{IP}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

2. Case
G
is type 1 and
H
finite étale.
Take a type 1 inverse system
G=\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}
. By
[
36
]
(Th. 2.2.2), for some
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
we have a commutative diagram with exact rows and cartesian right square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{n_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

Define
E_{n}
for
n\geq n_{0}
as the pullback of
E_{n_{0}}
and
G_{n}
over
G_{n_{0}}
. This way
E=\varprojlim_{n}E_{n}
, with
E_{n}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
E_{n+1}\to E_{n}
surjective. There is an exact sequence :

0\to H/H^{\prime}_{n}\to E_{n}/E_{n}^{0}\to G_{n}/G_{n}^{\prime}\to 0

where
H_{n}^{\prime}
is the inverse image of
E_{n}^{0}
in
H
, and
G_{n}^{\prime}
the image of
E_{n}^{0}
in
G_{n}
. Then
G_{n}/G_{n}^{\prime}
is finite étale as a quotient of the finite étale groups
H/H_{n}^{\prime}
and
E_{n}/E_{n}^{0}
, and connected as a quotient of the connected group
G_{n}
, hence
G_{n}/G_{n}^{\prime}=0
. Thus
G_{n}=G_{n}^{\prime}
meaning we also have an exact sequence :

0\to H^{\prime}_{n}\to E_{n}^{0}\to G_{n}\to 0

Now
(H^{\prime}_{n})_{n}
is a decreasing sequence of closed subgroups of the finite étale (thus Noetherian) group
H
, so for large
n\geq n_{0}
, it is constant at some
H^{\prime}\leq H
. We have commutative diagrams with exact rows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n+1}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{n+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{n}^{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

so
E_{n+1}^{0}\to E_{n}^{0}
has the same kernel and cokernel as
G_{n+1}\to G_{n}
. It follows that
E^{\prime}=\varprojlim_{n\geq n_{1}}E_{n}^{0}
is type 1. We have also seen that
E_{n}/E_{n}^{0}=H/H_{n}^{\prime}
, so taking the limit in
n
we have an exact sequence
0\to E^{\prime}\to E\to H/H^{\prime}\to 0
, and
E
is type 1.

3. Case
G
is type 2 and
H
finite étale.
Take a type 2 direct system
G=\varinjlim_{n}G_{n}
. Let
E_{n}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
be the pullback of
G_{n}
in
E
. Again define
H^{\prime}_{n}\leq H
as the inverse image of
E_{n}^{0}
in
H
. Then
(H_{n}^{\prime})
is an increasing sequence of reduced closed subgroups of the finite étale group
H
, so for large
n
is is constant at some
H^{\prime}\leq H
. As in the previous case we have exact sequences :

0\to H^{\prime}\to E_{n}^{0}\to G_{n}\to 0,\qquad 0\to E_{n}^{0}\to E_{n}\to H/H^{\prime}\to 0

because
\coker(E_{n}^{0}\to G_{n})=(E_{n}/E_{n}^{0})/(H/H_{n}^{\prime})
is simultaneously connected and finite étale, thus trivial. The snake lemma applied to the first sequence, for varying
n\geq n_{0}
, shows
E_{n}^{0}\to E_{n+1}^{0}
is injective thus
E^{\prime}=\varinjlim_{n\geq n_{0}}E_{n}^{0}
is type 2. Passing to the colimit in the second sequence shows
E^{\prime}
is a subgroup of
E=\varinjlim_{n\geq n_{0}}E_{n}
, with finite étale quotient
H/H^{\prime}
, thus
E
is in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

4. Case
G
is connected and
H
is finite étale.
Let
G^{\prime}\leq G
be a type 1 subgroup such that
G/G^{\prime}
is type 2. Let
E^{\prime}
be the inverse image of
G^{\prime}
in
E
, then we have a short exact sequence
0\to H\to E^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}\to 0
so by case
(2.)
,
E^{\prime}
admits a type 1 subgroup
E_{1}
such that
E^{\prime}/E_{1}
is finite étale. We have a short exact sequence :

0\to E^{\prime}/E_{1}\to E/E_{1}\to E/E^{\prime}\to 0

where
E/E^{\prime}=G/G^{\prime}
is type 2, so by case
(3.)
,
E/E_{1}
admits a type 2 subgroup such that the quotient is finite étale. Taking
E_{2}\leq E
to be the inverse image of such a subgroup in
E
, we have
E_{1}\leq E_{2}\leq E
where
E_{1}
is type 1,
E_{2}/E_{1}
is type 2, and
E/E_{2}
is finite étale. Thus
E
is in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

5. General case.
Let
G^{\prime}\leq G
and
H^{\prime}\leq H
be connected objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
with
G/G^{\prime}
and
H/H^{\prime}
finite étale. Let
E^{\prime}
be the inverse image of
G^{\prime}
in
E
. The exact sequence
0\to H/H^{\prime}\to E^{\prime}/H^{\prime}\to G^{\prime}\to 0
and case
(2.)
show
E^{\prime}/H^{\prime}
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{k}
. Hence
E^{\prime}
admits a subobject
E^{\prime\prime}
containing
H^{\prime}
such that
E^{\prime\prime}/H^{\prime}
is a connected object of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
and
(E^{\prime}/H^{\prime})/(E^{\prime\prime}/H^{\prime})
is finite étale. Then case
(1.)
shows
E^{\prime\prime}
is a connected object of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
. The exact sequence
E^{\prime}/E^{\prime\prime}\to E/E^{\prime\prime}\to E/E^{\prime}\to 0
and identifications
E/E^{\prime}=G/G^{\prime}
and
E^{\prime}/E^{\prime\prime}=(E^{\prime}/H^{\prime})/(E^{\prime\prime}/H^{\prime})
show
E/E^{\prime\prime}
is finite étale, proving
E
belongs to
\mathcal{W}_{k}
.
∎

Remark 38
.

Let
k
be a field of characteristic
p>0
such that
[k:k^{p}]<+\infty
. We can define
\mathcal{W}_{k}
identically by replacing "connected" and "finite étale
p
-primary" in Definition
35
with "split" and "wound", respectively. In the proof of Lemma
37
, case
(1.)
generalizes : split objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
are stable under extensions in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. However in
(2.-3.)
we use that quotients of finite étale groups are finite étale, and increasing sequences of RPAU subgroups of finite étale groups are stationary, neither of which extends to wound groups if
k
is imperfect : there exists an exact sequence
0\to H\to G\to Q\to 0
in
\Alg_{u}(k)
with
G
wound and
Q=\mathbb{G}_{a,k}

[
6
]
(Ex. B.2.3), and since
k
has infinite dimension over
\mathbb{F}_{p}
, there exists a strictly increasing sequence of closed subgroups
\{(\mathbb{Z}/p)^{n}\}_{n\geq 0}
in
\mathbb{G}_{a,k}
which after inverse image and relative perfection, gives an infinite strictly increasing sequence of smooth subgroups of
G
.

2.3
Ind-pro-RPAU groups as sheaves

Definition 39
.

Let
k
be a perfect field.

—

A
k
-algebra is
rational
if it is a finite product of perfections of finite type field extensions of
k
, and
indrational
if it is a filtered colimit of rational algebras. The
indrational étale (resp. proétale) site

k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
(resp.
k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
) is the opposite of the category of indrational
k
-algebras with all
k
-algebra morphisms, with the étale (resp. proétale) topology.

—

A
k
-algebra is
perfect Artinian
if it is a finite product of (arbitrary) perfect field extensions of
k
. The
perfect Artinian étale site

k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
is (the opposite of) the category of perfect Artinian
k
-algebras with all
k
-algebra morphisms, with the étale topology.

—

A family of morphisms
\{R\to R_{i}\}_{i}
with perfect
k
-algebras
R,R_{i}
is a pro-fppf covering if each
R_{i}
is a filtered colimit of perfections of flat finitely presented
R
-algebras, and
R\to\prod_{i}R_{i}
is faithfully flat. The
perfect pro-fppf site

k_{\mathrm{profppf}}^{\mathrm{perf}}
is (the opposite of) the category of perfect
k
-algebras with all
k
-algebra morphisms, with the topology defined by pro-fppf coverings.

We write
f_{k}:k_{\mathrm{profppf}}^{\mathrm{perf}}\to k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
,
g_{k}:k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}\to k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
and
h_{k}=g\circ f
the premorphisms of sites defined by the identity. Finally we define the
change of site functor

\alpha_{k}=Rf_{k,*}Lh^{*}_{k}:D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})\to D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
.

See
[
38
]
(§3.1),
[
37
]
(§3),
[
36
]
(§2.1) for references on these sites. Compared to them, we commit a slight abuse of notations in writing sites
e.g.

k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
instead of
\Spec k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
.
k^{\mathrm{indrat}}_{\mathrm{et}}
with
k^{\prime}\in k^{\mathrm{indrat}}
; we write
R\Gamma((-)_{\mathrm{proet}},F)
and
R\Gamma((-)_{\mathrm{et}},F)
to differentiate between the resulting cohomologies. Note that the other sites in this paper (
S_{\mathrm{et}}
,
S_{\mathrm{Et}}
,
S_{\mathrm{RP}}
,
S_{\mathrm{RPS}}
,
k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
,
*_{\mathrm{proet}}
) are only ever viewed with one topology and we write
R\Gamma(U,-)
unambiguously for their objects
U
.

Definition 40
.

Let
k
be a perfect field.

—

We write
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
,
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
and
D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
the full subcategories objects of
D^{b}(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
,
D^{b}(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
and
D^{b}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
with cohomology in (or representable by objects of)
\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

—

We write
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}
,
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}
and
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
the smallest full triangulated subcategories of
D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
,
D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
and
D^{b}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
closed under taking split subobjects, and which contain (objects representable by) objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
in degree
0
.

The reason for defining these sites is that they properly capture ind-pro-RPAU groups over
k
, a statement made precise in the following Proposition
41
. Our eventual interest among these lies in
k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
, which understands all of
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. The site
k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
is intermediary : it captures
\mathcal{W}_{k}
and gives more straightforward computations before passing to
k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
via the change of site functor. Many of the results we use rely on the study of
k_{\mathrm{profppf}}^{\mathrm{perf}}
, but we will not work with it directly  ; we mention it only to define
\alpha_{k}
.

Proposition 41
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
.

1.

The Yoneda functor induces fully faithful, exact functors of abelian and triangulated categories :

\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}),\qquad D^{b}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))\to D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}).

identifying
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
as a full abelian subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
closed under extensions and
D^{b}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
as a full triangulated category of
D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
.

2.

The Yoneda functor induces fully faithful, exact functors of exact and triangulated categories :

\mathcal{W}_{k}\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}),\qquad\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}\to D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})

identifying
\mathcal{W}_{k}
as a full subcategory of
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
closed under extensions, and induce an exact equivalence of triangulated categories
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}\cong\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}
. We have commutative squares :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(k_{\mathrm{RP}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sh(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}(\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(k_{\mathrm{RP}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subseteq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w_{*}}

where
w:k_{\mathrm{RP}}\to k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
is the premorphism of sites induced by identity.

3.

The change of site
\alpha_{k}:D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})\to D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
induces a commutative square of exact equivalences :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}

4.

For all
k^{\prime}\in k^{\mathrm{perar}}_{\mathrm{et}}
and
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}
, we have
R\Gamma(k^{\prime}_{\mathrm{proet}},\alpha_{k}G)=R\Gamma(k^{\prime}_{\mathrm{et}},G)
. If additionally
H^{q}(G)\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
then
H^{q}(\alpha_{k}G)\in\Sh(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
and
H^{q}(G)\in\Sh(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
are representable by the same object of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
. In particular,
\alpha_{k}
also induces a commutative square of equivalences :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}

Proof of Proposition
41
.

1.
See
[
36
]
(Prop.2.3.4) for the second functor’s full faithfulness. The first’s follows because
\Hom_{D(k^{\mathrm{indrat}}_{\mathrm{proet}})}(G,H)=\Hom_{\Sh(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})}(G,H)
for
G,H\in\Sh(k^{\mathrm{indrat}}_{\mathrm{proet}})
concentrated in degree
0
. The stability of
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
under extensions in
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
follows from the isomorphism, for
G,H\in\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
:

\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}(G,H)=\Hom_{D^{b}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))}(G,H[1])=\Hom_{D(k^{\mathrm{indrat}}_{\mathrm{proet}})}(G,H[1])=\Ext^{1}_{\Sh(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})}(G,H).

3.
See
[
39
]
(Prop.3.1.5).

4.
See
[
39
]
(Prop. 3.1.4) and
[
40
]
(Prop. 2.1).

2.
For the first part, the inclusion
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}\to D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
follows from
(1.)
and
(3.)
, the inclusion
\mathcal{W}_{k}\to\Sh(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
follows as in
(1.)
, and the stability of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
under extensions in
\Sh(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
follows as in
(1.)
from its stability under extensions in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
(Lemma
37
). For the commutative squares, note that
w_{*}
takes a sheaf to its restriction (as a presheaf) to the full subcategory
k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
. As such it commutes with the Yoneda functor on the underived level. Since both it and the Yoneda functor are exact, they commute on the derived level as well.
∎

2.4
Serre duality and duality for
\mathcal{W}_{k}

There is a suitable notion of duality for many ind-pro-RPAU groups and especially objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
, which generalizes in an intuitive way the duality for RPAU groups (Theorem
26
). This is referred to as Serre duality in
[
36
]
.

Consider the functors
(-)^{0}:\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
\pi_{0}:\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
of neutral component and component group. Their ind-pro-extensions are again written
(-)^{0},\pi_{0}:\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)\to\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Namely :

G^{0}=\varinjlim_{i}\varprojlim_{j}G_{ij}^{0},\qquad\pi_{0}(G)=\varinjlim_{i}\varprojlim_{j}\pi_{0}(G_{ij}).

whenever
G_{ij}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
G=\varinjlim_{i}\varprojlim_{j}G_{ij}
. The natural maps
(-)^{0}\to\mathrm{id}\to\pi_{0}
extend to the ind-pro-functors similarly. We will say
G
is
connected
if
\pi_{0}(G)=0
and
ind-pro-finite étale
if
G^{0}=0
.

Lemma 42
.

Consider
G\in\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
.

1.

We have an exact sequence
0\to G^{0}\to G\to\pi_{0}(G)\to 0
formed by the natural maps, functorial in
G
. In particular
G
is connected (resp. ind-pro-finite étale) if and only if
G=G^{0}
(resp.
G=\pi_{0}(G)
).

2.

If
G\to H
is a surjection in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
G
is connected then
H
is connected. If
H\to G
is an injection in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and
G
is ind-pro-finite étale, then
H
is ind-pro-finite-étale.

3.

If
G
is connected and
H
is ind-pro-finite étale, then any morphism
G\to H
is trivial.

4.

The object
G^{0}
is the unique subobject of
G
which is connected with ind-pro-finite étale quotient.

5.

If
G\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
then
G^{0}
is connected in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
(in the sense of Definition
35
) and
\pi_{0}(G)
is finite étale.

Proof of Lemma
42
.

1.
This follows from the exactness of cofiltered limits in
\mathrm{PAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
and of filtered colimits in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Functoriality follows from the naturality of
(-)^{0}\to\mathrm{id}\to\pi_{0}
.

2.
From
(1.)
, any surjection
G\to H
(resp. injection
H\to G
) induces a surjection
\pi_{0}(G)\to\pi_{0}(H)
(resp. injection
H^{0}\to G^{0}
). The claim follows from the definition of connected and ind-pro-finite étale.

3.
As
G
is connected, the map factors as
G=G^{0}\to H^{0}\to H
by functoriality of
(-)^{0}
, and
H^{0}=0
.

4.
For
H\leq G
a subobject in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
with
H
connected and
G/H
ind-pro-finite étale, as in
(3.)
we have a map
H=H^{0}\to G^{0}
which induces
G/H\to G/G^{0}=\pi_{0}(G)
, and we have a map
\pi_{0}(G)\to\pi_{0}(G/H)=G/H
. These maps exhibit an isomorphism
G/H=G/G^{0}
, hence
H=G^{0}
as subobjects of
G
.

5.
There exists
G^{(0)}\subseteq G
connected in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
such that
G/G^{(0)}
is finite étale. Then
G/G^{(0)}
is in particular ind-pro-finite étale, so by
(4.)
it suffices to show
G^{(0)}
is connected in
\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
. Consider an exact sequence
0\to G^{(1)}\to G^{(0)}\to G^{(2)}\to 0
with
G^{(1)}
type 1 and
G^{(2)}
type 2. Given type 1 and 2 systems
G^{(1)}=\varprojlim_{m}G^{(1)}_{m}
and
G^{(2)}=\varinjlim_{n}G^{(2)}_{n}
, consider a following commutative diagram with exact rows :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(0)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(2)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(1)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(0)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(2)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(1)}_{m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(0)}_{n,m}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G^{(2)}_{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}

with cartesian upper-right square and cocartesian lower-left square. Then
G^{(0)}_{n,m}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is connected as an extension of connected
k
-groups. Hence
G^{(0)}=\varinjlim_{n}\varprojlim_{m}G^{(0)}_{n,m}
is connected.
∎

Theorem 43
.

Let
k
be a perfect field of characteristic
p>0
. Let
S_{k}
be one of the sites
k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
or
k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
. We define the
Serre duality
functor as
(-)^{\vee}_{S_{k}}=R\HOM_{S_{k}}(-,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}):D(S_{k})\to D(S_{k})
.

1.

For
S_{k}=k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}},k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}
, the functor
(-)^{\vee}_{S_{k}}
sends
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{S_{k}}
to itself and we have a commutative square :

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{k}}

where
\alpha_{k}:D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})\to D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
is the change of site functor. Thus
(-)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}
and
(-)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}
restrict via the Yoneda embeddings to the same functor on
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
, simply denoted
(-)^{\vee}
.

2.

For
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
, the natural map
G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism. In other words, the pairing
G\otimes^{L}G^{\vee}\to\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}
is perfect when viewed in either
D(k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}})
or
D(k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}})
.

3.

For
G\in D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
, the dual
G^{\vee}
is computed as follows.

(a)

If
G\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
,
G^{\vee}=w_{*}R\HOM_{k_{\mathrm{RP}}}(G,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
where
w:k_{\mathrm{RP}}\to k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}
is induced by identity.

(b)

If
G\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
is connected then
G^{\vee}
is concentrated in degree
1
and
\EXT^{1}(G,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is a connected object of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
. The functor
\EXT^{1}(-,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
sends type 1 systems to type 2 systems and vice versa.

(c)

If
G\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
,
H^{0}(G^{\vee})=\HOM(\pi_{0}(G),\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is finite étale,
H^{1}(G^{\vee})^{0}=\EXT^{1}(G^{0},\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is connected in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
, and
H^{q}(G^{\vee})=0
for
q\neq 0,1
. In particular,
G^{\vee}\in D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
.

(d)

If
G\in D^{b}_{\mathcal{W}_{k}}(\mathrm{IPAlg}_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k))
and
H=G^{\vee}
, then for all
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we have isomorphisms :

H^{q}(G)^{0}=\EXT^{1}(H^{1-q}(H)^{0},\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\qquad\pi_{0}(H^{q}(G))=\HOM(\pi_{0}(H^{-q}(H)),\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p}).

We say
H^{q}(G)^{0}
and
H^{1-q}(H)^{0}
(resp.
\pi_{0}(H^{q}(G))
and
\pi_{0}(H^{-q}(H))
) satisfy a perfect Serre duality of connected (resp. finite étale) objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

Proof of Theorem
43
.

1.
That
\alpha_{k}(G^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}})=(\alpha_{k}G)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}
for
G\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
is
[
39
]
(Prop. 3.1.8). The fact that
(-)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{et}}^{\mathrm{perar}}}
and
(-)^{\vee}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}
send
\mathcal{W}_{k}
to
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IP}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
, hence
\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IP}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
to itself, is part of
(3.c)
below.

2.
If
G=\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}
is a type 1 inverse system then by
(3.b)
below,
G^{\vee}
is concentrated in degree
1
with
H^{1}(G^{\vee})=\varinjlim_{n}H^{1}(G_{n}^{\vee})
a type 2 direct system, and
(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is concentrated in degree
0
with
H^{0}((G^{\vee})^{\vee})=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}(H^{1}(G_{n}^{\vee})^{\vee})
. By
(3.a)
and Theorem
26
.2 since each
G_{n}
is connected hence split (because
k
is perfect), the maps
G_{n}\to H^{1}(H^{1}(G_{n}^{\vee})^{\vee})
are isomorphisms. Thus
G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism. If
G
is type 2, by an identical argument
G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism. If
G
is finite étale
p
-primary, by
(3.a)
and Theorem
26
.1 the morphism
G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism.

Objects of
\mathcal{W}_{k}
are extensions of type 1, type 2 and finite étale
p
-primary groups, so by exactness of
(-)^{\vee}
, the natural map
G\to(G^{\vee})^{\vee}
is an isomorphism for
G\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
, and in turn for
G\in\langle\mathcal{W}_{k}\rangle_{\mathrm{IP}\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)}
.

3.a.
This is part of
[
36
]
(Prop. 2.4.1.b).

3.b.
Let
G=\varprojlim_{n}G_{n}
be a type 1 inverse system. By
[
36
]
(Th. 2.3.3.(a)), for
q\in\mathbb{Z}
we have :

H^{q}(G^{\vee})=\EXT^{q}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}(G,\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})=\varinjlim_{n}\varinjlim_{m}\EXT^{q}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}(G_{n},\mathbb{Z}/p^{m})

where by
(3.a)
,
\varinjlim_{m}\EXT^{q}(G_{n},\mathbb{Z}/p^{m})=H^{q}(G_{n}^{\vee})
. Hence
H^{q}(G^{\vee})=\varinjlim_{n}H^{q}(G_{n}^{\vee})
. By Theorem
26
.2,
H^{q}(G_{n}^{\vee})
is in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
, trivial if
q\neq 1
, and connected if
q=0
. Thus
H^{q}(G^{\vee})=0
for
q\neq 1
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have an exact sequence :

0\to N_{n}\to G_{n+1}\to G_{n}\to 0

where
N_{n}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is some connected group by the type 1 assumption, so taking the long exact sequence of Ext’s and applying the above yields an exact sequence :

0\to H^{1}(G^{\vee}_{n})\to H^{1}(G^{\vee}_{n+1})\to H^{1}(N_{n}^{\vee})\to 0

showing that
H^{1}(G^{\vee}_{n})\to H^{1}(G^{\vee}_{n+1})
is injective, and
H^{1}(G^{\vee})=\varinjlim_{n}H^{q}(G_{n}^{\vee})
is a type 2 direct system.

Let
G=\varinjlim_{n}G_{n}
be a type 2 direct system. Again by
[
36
]
(Th. 2.3.3.(a)) we have a spectral sequence :

E^{ij}_{2}=(R^{i}\varprojlim_{n})H^{j}(G_{n}^{\vee})\Rightarrow\EXT^{i+j}_{k_{\mathrm{proet}}^{\mathrm{indrat}}}(G,\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Z})=H^{i+j}(G^{\vee})

where as before,
H^{j}(G_{n}^{\vee})
is trivial if
j\neq 1
, and connected in
\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
if
j=1
. Thus by
[
36
]
(Prop. 2.1.2.f),
E^{ij}_{2}=0
in all cases except
i=0
and
j=1
. This shows
H^{q}(G^{\vee})=0
if
q\neq 1
, and
H^{1}(G^{\vee})=\varprojlim_{n}H^{1}(G_{n}^{\vee})
. For
n\in\mathbb{N}
we have an exact sequence :

0\to G_{n}\to G_{n+1}\to Q_{n}\to 0

where
Q_{n}\in\Alg_{u}^{\mathrm{RP}}(k)
is some connected group as a quotient of a connected group, so taking the long exact sequence of Ext’s and applying
(5.b)
yields an exact sequence :

0\to H^{1}(Q^{\vee}_{n})\to H^{1}(G^{\vee}_{n+1})\to H^{1}(G^{\vee}_{n})\to 0

where
H^{1}(Q^{\vee}_{n})
is connected, so
H^{1}(G^{\vee})=\varinjlim_{n}H^{1}(G_{n})
is a type 1 inverse system.

If
G\in\mathcal{W}_{k}
is connected, writing
G
as an extension of type 1 and type 2 groups and taking the long exact sequence of Ext’s shows
H^{q}(G^{\vee})=0
if
q\neq 1
, and
H^{1}(G^{\vee})
is connected in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
.

3.c.
This is obtained by taking the long exact sequence of Ext’s for
0\to G^{0}\to G\to\pi_{0}(G)\to 0
and noting that, by
(3.a)
and Theorem
26
.2,
\pi_{0}(G)^{\vee}
is concentrated in degree
0
.

3.d.
This follows from
(5.e)
as the spectral sequence :

E^{ij}_{2}=\EXT^{i}(H^{-j}(H),\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})\Rightarrow H^{i+j}(G)

degenerates into short exact sequences :

0\to\EXT^{1}(H^{1-q}(H),\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to H^{q}(G)\to\HOM(H^{-q}(H),\mathbb{Q}_{p}/\mathbb{Z}_{p})\to 0

whose first term is connected in
\mathcal{W}_{k}
and whose third term is finite étale, by
(3.c)
.
∎

3
Ind-pro-finite groups

3.1
Ind-pro-finite groups as condensed groups

Definition 44
.

Let
S
be a fixed set of primes.

—

We write
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}
the category of finite abelian groups
G
such that the
p
-torsion of
G
is trivial for all primes
p\notin S
. When
S
is the set of all primes, write
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}=\mathrm{Ab}_{\mathrm{fin}}
the category of all finite abelian groups. When
S=\{p\}
has one element, write
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}=\mathrm{Ab}_{p}
the category of finite abelian
p
-groups.

—

Define
\mathcal{W}_{S}\subseteq\mathrm{I}\mathrm{PAb}_{S}
as the full subcategory of objects
G\in\mathrm{IPAb}_{S}
such that there exists an exact sequence
0\to G^{\prime}\to G\to G^{\prime\prime}\to 0
where
G^{\prime}\in\mathrm{PAb}_{S}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-indexed limit of objects of
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}
, and
G^{\prime\prime}\in\mathrm{IAb}_{S}
is an
\mathbb{N}
-indexed colimit of objects of
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}
. Similarly we write
\mathcal{W}_{S}=\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{fin}}
if
S
is the set of all primes and
\mathcal{W}_{S}=\mathcal{W}_{p}
if
S=\{p\}
.

Note that
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}=\bigoplus_{p\in S}\mathrm{Ab}_{p}
and
D^{b}(\mathrm{Ab}_{p})=\bigoplus_{p\in S}D^{b}(\mathrm{Ab}_{p})
, as
\Ext^{i}(A,B)=0
for
i\geq 0
if
A\in\mathrm{Ab}_{p}
and
B\in\mathrm{Ab}_{q}
with primes
p\neq q
. The same decomposition holds for
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Ab}_{S}
and
\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Ab}_{S}
if
S
is finite, but in general
\mathrm{IP}\mathrm{Ab}_{S}
is larger than
\bigoplus_{p\in S}\mathrm{IPAb}_{p}
. For instance,
\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Ab}_{\mathrm{fin}}\cong\prod_{p}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Ab}_{p}
is the category of torsion abelian groups, but
\bigoplus_{p}\mathrm{I}\mathrm{Ab}_{p}
is the category of finite exponent abelian groups.

Definition 45
.

Let
S
be a fixed set of primes.

—

The
proétale site of the point

*_{\mathrm{proet}}
is the category of pro-finite spaces with coverings given by jointly surjective finite families of continuous maps. A
condensed group
is an object of
\Sh(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
.

—

We write
\mathrm{TopAb}
the category of topological abelian groups with continuous morphisms, and
\mathrm{LCAb}
the full subcategory of locally compact Hausdorff topological abelian groups.

—

Consider three functors :

Y:\mathrm{IPAb}_{S}\to\Sh(*_{\mathrm{proet}}),\qquad(-)_{\mathrm{top}}:\mathrm{IPAb}_{S}\to\mathrm{TopAb},\qquad(-)_{\mathrm{cond}}:\mathrm{TopAb}\to\Sh(*_{\mathrm{proet}})

respectively given as the ind-completion of the Yoneda functor
\mathrm{PAb}_{S}\to\Sh(*_{\mathrm{proet}})
; the ind-pro-completion of the functor
\mathrm{Ab}_{S}\to\mathrm{TopAb}
giving the discrete topology to a finite group  ; by
G_{\mathrm{cond}}(X)
being the set of continuous maps
X\to G
for
X
pro-finite and
G\in\mathrm{TopAb}
.

—

We say a sequence
0\to A\xrightarrow{v}B\xrightarrow{u}C\to 0
in
\mathrm{TopAb}
is
topologically exact
if
u\circ v=0
,
v
is an immersion, and the map
B/A\to C
induced by
u
is a homeomorphism.

See
[
5
]
for more on
*_{\mathrm{proet}}
and condensed groups  ; our
(-)_{\mathrm{cond}}
is written
G\mapsto\underline{G}
there. Equivalently,
0\to A\xrightarrow{v}B\xrightarrow{u}C\to 0
is topologically exact if it is exact,
v
is an immersion, and
u
is open. In the literature, such a sequence is also called
strictly exact
. This endows
\mathrm{TopAb}
and
\mathrm{LCAb}
with exact structures.

Remark 46
.

The category
\mathrm{TopAb}
has all limits, given by computing the limit in
\mathrm{Ab}
and equipping it with the initial topology with respect to the projections. The category
\mathrm{TopAb}
also has all colimits, and the object of
\mathrm{Ab}
underlying a colimit
G=\mathrm{colim}_{i}G_{i}
in
\mathrm{TopAb}
is the same colimit computed
\mathrm{Ab}
. The topology on
G
is given as the initial topology with respect to (an essentially small, appropriate class of) maps
G\to H
with
H\in\mathrm{TopAb}
such that
G_{i}\to H
is continuous for all
i
’s. This topology is hard to pin down, and usually different from the the final topology with respect to the maps
G_{i}\to G
: the formation of the final topology on a colimit of sets does not commute with products in general, so
G
equipped with the final topology is not generally a topological group. However, if the colimit is indexed by a countable directed set, and the
G_{i}
’s are locally compact Hausdorff, then
\mathrm{colim}_{i}(G_{i}\times G_{i})=(\mathrm{colim}_{i}G_{i})\times(\mathrm{colim}_{i}G_{i})
in the category of topological spaces, and the colimits in
\mathrm{TopAb}
and in topological spaces coincide in this case
[
14
]
(Th. 4.1). Thus for
G_{i}\in\mathrm{P}\mathrm{Ab}_{\mathrm{fin}}
,
G=\varinjlim_{i}G_{i}\in\mathrm{IPAb}_{\mathrm{fin}}
, if the index category for
i
is a countable directed set, then the topology on the colimit
G_{\mathrm{top}}=\varinjlim_{i}(G_{i})_{\mathrm{top}}
is the same as the final topology with respect to the maps
(G_{i})_{\mathrm{top}}\to G_{\mathrm{top}}
. This in particular applies to
G\in\mathcal{W}_{\mathrm{fin}}
.

Lemma 47
.

For
S
a set of primes and
G\to H
a surjection in
\mathrm{PAb}_{S}
, the map
G_{\mathrm{top}}\to H_{\mathrm{top}}
is surjective.

Proof of Lemma
47
.

Write
N=\ker(G\to H)\in\mathrm{PAb}_{S}
, and choose cofiltered systems
N=\varprojlim_{i}N_{i}
and
H=\varprojlim_{j}H_{j}
.
Let
G_{i}
be the pushout of
G
along
N\to N_{i}
, so we have exact sequences
0\to N_{i}\to G_{i}\to H\to 0
and
G=\varprojlim_{i}G_{i}
. By
[
36
]
(Prop. 2.2.2), we have
\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{PAb}_{S}}(H,N_{i})=\varinjlim_{j}\Ext^{1}_{\mathrm{PAb}_{S}}(H_{j},N_{i})
so for some
j_{0}
there exists an exact sequence
